```
1005	  async uploadFile(
1006	    folderIpnsName: string,
1007	    data: Uint8Array,
1008	    fileName: string,
1009	    mimeType: string,
1010	    onProgress?: ProgressCallback
1011	  ): Promise<{ cid: string }> {
1012	    return this.withOperation('uploadFile', async () => {
1013	      const folder = await this.requireFolder(folderIpnsName);
1014	
1015	      if (folder.children.some((child) => child.name === fileName)) {
1016	        throw new Error('An item with this name already exists');
1017	      }
1018	
1019	      const fileId = crypto.randomUUID();
1020	
1021	      // Build BYO-IPFS pinFn override when mode is not 'cipherbox'
1022	      const mode = this.config.pinningConfig?.mode ?? 'cipherbox';
1023	      let secondaryWarning: string | undefined;
1024	      const pinFn =
1025	        mode === 'cipherbox' || !this.externalProvider
1026	          ? undefined
1027	          : async (
1028	              ctx: SdkContext,
1029	              encData: Uint8Array,
1030	              prog?: ProgressCallback
1031	            ): Promise<{ cid: string; size: number }> => {
1032	              const result = await this.pinWithMode(encData, ctx, prog);
1033	              secondaryWarning = result.secondaryWarning;
1034	              return { cid: result.cid, size: result.size };
1035	            };
1036	
1037	      // 1. Encrypt and upload file, create file metadata
1038	      const encryptionMode = selectEncryptionMode(mimeType, data.length);
1039	      const uploadResult = await sdkCore.uploadFile({
1040	        data,
1041	        fileId,
1042	        mimeType,
1043	        folderKey: folder.folderKey,
1044	        userPublicKey: this.config.vaultKeypair.publicKey,
1045	        ctx: this.ctx,
1046	        onProgress,
1047	        teeKeys: this.config.teeKeys,
1048	        pinFn,
1049	        encryptionMode,
1050	      });
1051	
1052	      try {
1053	        // 2. Add FilePointer to folder's children
1054	        const baseChildren = [...folder.children];
1055	        const { updatedChildren } = sdkCore.addFilePointerToFolder({
1056	          children: folder.children,
1057	          fileId,
1058	          fileName,
1059	          fileMetaIpnsName: uploadResult.fileMetaIpnsName,
1060	          ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted: uploadResult.ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted,
1061	        });
1062	
1063	        // 3. Concurrent: file IPNS batch publish + folder metadata update
1064	        //    These two operations are independent -- no data dependency between them.
1065	        //    Using Promise.allSettled to handle partial failures gracefully.
1066	        const [batchResult, folderResult] = await Promise.allSettled([
1067	          sdkCore.batchPublishIpnsRecords([uploadResult.ipnsRecord], this.ctx),
1068	          sdkCore.updateFolderMetadataAndPublish({
1069	            children: updatedChildren,
1070	            baseChildren,
1071	            folderKey: folder.folderKey,
1072	            ipnsPrivateKey: folder.ipnsKeypair.privateKey,
1073	            ipnsName: folderIpnsName,
1074	            sequenceNumber: folder.sequenceNumber,
1075	            ctx: this.ctx,
1076	          }),
1077	        ]);
1078	
1079	        // Folder metadata update is critical -- must succeed for upload to be valid
1080	        if (folderResult.status === 'rejected') {
1081	          throw folderResult.reason;
1082	        }
1083	
1084	        // File IPNS batch publish failure is non-critical -- the FilePointer in
1085	        // folder metadata is valid, and the file IPNS record will be created
1086	        // on next publish attempt or TEE republish cycle
1087	        // Note: if folder update fails but batch publish succeeded, an orphaned IPNS
1088	        // record may exist. This is benign -- no FilePointer references it, and the
1089	        // IPNS name will be reused on the next successful upload attempt.
1090	        if (batchResult.status === 'rejected') {
1091	          const batchError =
1092	            batchResult.reason instanceof Error
1093	              ? batchResult.reason
1094	              : new Error(String(batchResult.reason));
1095	          console.warn(
1096	            '[SDK] File IPNS batch publish failed (non-critical, will retry on next publish):',
1097	            batchError
1098	          );
1099	          this.emitter.emit({
1100	            type: 'ipns:batchPublishFailed',
1101	            ipnsNames: [uploadResult.ipnsRecord.ipnsName],
1102	            error: batchError,
1103	          });
1104	        } else if (batchResult.value?.totalFailed > 0) {
1105	          // Server accepted the request but reported partial failure for some records
1106	          console.warn(
1107	            `[SDK] File IPNS batch publish partially failed: ${batchResult.value.totalFailed} of ${batchResult.value.totalFailed + batchResult.value.totalSucceeded} records failed`
1108	          );
1109	          this.emitter.emit({
1110	            type: 'ipns:batchPublishFailed',
1111	            ipnsNames: [uploadResult.ipnsRecord.ipnsName],
1112	            error: new Error(
1113	              `Batch publish partial failure: ${batchResult.value.totalFailed} record(s) failed`
1114	            ),
```

> AGENT

Checked the actual scenario code. Your instinct is right to ask — but the premise is already handled: **these scenarios go through the SDK, not raw endpoints.** Here's exactly what hits the wire.

## What surface each scenario uses

The pool (`client-pool.ts:70`) provisions **real accounts** via `createTestAccount` (the full 5-step sdk-e2e flow) — each virtual client is a genuine authenticated `CipherBoxClient` with its own vault, root IPNS keypair, and folder key. Then the workloads drive the **high-level SDK client methods**, same surface the desktop/web app uses:

- **`upload-throughput`** → `runFileWorkload` calls `client.uploadFile(...)` (`client.ts:1005`). One "uploadFile" metric is **not** a raw `POST /ipfs/upload` — it fans out to the whole real-user pipeline: encrypt → pin ciphertext → pin file metadata → `Promise.allSettled([batchPublishIpnsRecords, updateFolderMetadataAndPublish])` → folder-meta pin + folder IPNS publish. ~4–5 endpoints incl. the IPNS publish bottleneck. The scenario comment says it outright: *"including IPNS publish per file."*
- **`mixed-workload`** → `client.createFolder / uploadFile / renameItem / moveItem / deleteItem` with weighted-random mix (4:2:1:1:1) and live folder-tree state. **This is the "bunch of real users doing realistic things" simulation you're describing.**
- **`sustained-load`** → steady 2 ops/sec/client of create→upload→delete for 300s — catches leaks / connection-pool exhaustion / time-based degradation.

So the SDK-driven real-user simulation already exists and is what's dispatchable against staging.

## Where you're actually right — two real gaps

1. **It's write-path-heavy.** `upload-throughput` sets `verifyDownloads: false`, and the dispatchable set barely exercises **reads** (resolve/fetch/decrypt), versioning, or sharing. If your recent API changes touched read or share paths, none of the staging-dispatchable scenarios will catch a regression there.

2. **The single-surface isolation scenarios aren't wired to staging.** There's a second family — `sdk-upload-pipeline`, `sdk-folder-read`, `sdk-ipns-contention` — that calls `sdk-core` functions **directly** (`sdkCore.uploadFile`, `createAndPublishIpnsRecord`, `loadFolderMetadata`), bypassing `CipherBoxClient` to isolate one bottleneck (e.g. pure read path, pure IPNS contention). These are **not in the workflow's `scenario` dropdown** — only the 5 non-byo + 3 byo are. To run them against staging you'd add the name to the `choice` list (one-line edit; vitest matches by filename substring) or run locally with `LOAD_TEST_API_URL` pointed at staging.

## Recommendation

For "did recent API changes regress perf across realistic usage," run this trio sequentially against staging (per the no-concurrent-runs rule):

1. **`mixed-workload`** @ 50/100/200 — realistic CRUD mix (the real-user sim)
2. **`sustained-load`** @ 200 — steady-state; compare directly to `staging-sustained-load.json` (uploadFile p95 11.6s, createFolder p95 11.9s, 0.17% errors)
3. **`upload-throughput`** @ 50/100/200 — write-path number vs the 19.2 staging CI table (15.10/18.76/19.22 ops/s)

Scenario choice should track what your changes touched. Want me to **diff the recent API commits first** to confirm the trio covers the changed surface (and flag if I should add `sdk-folder-read` to the workflow for read-path coverage) — then fire them in order, watch the runs, and diff the metrics artifacts against the baselines?

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/scripts/staging-loadtest-sweep.sh",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# Sequential staging load-test sweep.\n# Dispatches load-test.yml runs one at a time (never concurrent -> respects the\n# \"no concurrent staging load tests\" rule), polls each to completion, and\n# downloads its metrics artifact. NOT committed; scratch tooling for a one-off sweep.\nset -uo pipefail\n\nREPO=\"FSM1/cipher-box\"\nWF_FILE=\"load-test.yml\"\nWF_ID=\"249928808\"\nOUTDIR=\"${1:?usage: staging-loadtest-sweep.sh <outdir>}\"\nLOG=\"$OUTDIR/progress.log\"\nSUMMARY=\"$OUTDIR/summary.tsv\"\nmkdir -p \"$OUTDIR\"\n\nlog() { echo \"[$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)] $*\" | tee -a \"$LOG\"; }\ngh_retry() { local i; for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6; do env -u GITHUB_TOKEN gh \"$@\" 2>>\"$LOG\" && return 0; sleep 5; done; return 1; }\n\nruns=(\n  \"mixed-workload:50\"\n  \"mixed-workload:100\"\n  \"mixed-workload:200\"\n  \"sustained-load:200\"\n  \"upload-throughput:50\"\n  \"upload-throughput:100\"\n  \"upload-throughput:200\"\n)\n\nprintf 'scenario\\tclients\\trun_id\\tconclusion\\tartifact_dir\\n' > \"$SUMMARY\"\nlog \"=== Staging load-test sweep: ${#runs[@]} runs against $REPO ===\"\n\nfor spec in \"${runs[@]}\"; do\n  scenario=\"${spec%%:*}\"; clients=\"${spec##*:}\"\n  label=\"$scenario @ ${clients} clients\"\n  log \"--- Dispatching: $label ---\"\n\n  before=\"$(gh_retry api \"repos/$REPO/actions/workflows/$WF_ID/runs?per_page=1\" --jq '.workflow_runs[0].id' || echo 0)\"\n\n  if ! gh_retry workflow run \"$WF_FILE\" -f environment=staging -f scenario=\"$scenario\" -f client_count=\"$clients\"; then\n    log \"!! DISPATCH FAILED for $label -- skipping\"\n    printf '%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\n' \"$scenario\" \"$clients\" \"-\" \"dispatch_failed\" \"-\" >> \"$SUMMARY\"\n    continue\n  fi\n\n  runid=\"\"\n  for _ in $(seq 1 36); do\n    sleep 5\n    latest=\"$(gh_retry api \"repos/$REPO/actions/workflows/$WF_ID/runs?per_page=1\" --jq '.workflow_runs[0].id' || echo 0)\"\n    if [ \"$latest\" != \"$before\" ] && [ \"$latest\" != \"0\" ]; then runid=\"$latest\"; break; fi\n  done\n  if [ -z \"$runid\" ]; then\n    log \"!! Could not capture run id for $label -- skipping\"\n    printf '%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\n' \"$scenario\" \"$clients\" \"-\" \"no_run_id\" \"-\" >> \"$SUMMARY\"\n    continue\n  fi\n  log \"Run id $runid ($label) -> https://github.com/$REPO/actions/runs/$runid\"\n\n  status=\"queued\"\n  while [ \"$status\" != \"completed\" ]; do\n    sleep 20\n    status=\"$(gh_retry api \"repos/$REPO/actions/runs/$runid\" --jq '.status' || echo unknown)\"\n  done\n  concl=\"$(gh_retry api \"repos/$REPO/actions/runs/$runid\" --jq '.conclusion' || echo unknown)\"\n  log \"Run $runid completed: conclusion=$concl\"\n\n  rundir=\"$OUTDIR/${scenario}-${clients}c\"\n  mkdir -p \"$rundir\"\n  if gh_retry run download \"$runid\" -R \"$REPO\" -D \"$rundir\"; then\n    log \"Artifacts -> $rundir\"\n  else\n    log \"No artifact downloaded for $label (conclusion=$concl)\"\n  fi\n  printf '%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\t%s\\n' \"$scenario\" \"$clients\" \"$runid\" \"$concl\" \"$rundir\" >> \"$SUMMARY\"\ndone\n\nlog \"=== SWEEP COMPLETE ===\"\n"
}
```

```
1	/**
2	 * Mixed Workload Scenario
3	 *
4	 * Simulates realistic usage: folder CRUD, file uploads, renames, moves, deletes
5	 * with weighted probabilities. Tests the system under representative load.
6	 */
7	
8	import { describe, it, afterAll } from 'vitest';
9	import {
10	  createClientPool,
11	  destroyClientPool,
12	  aggregateAndReport,
13	  type PoolClient,
14	} from '../harness/client-pool';
15	import { expectThresholdsPassed, type ThresholdConfig } from '../harness/thresholds';
16	import { runMixedWorkload } from '../workloads/mixed-workload';
17	
18	const NUM_CLIENTS = parseInt(process.env.LOAD_TEST_CLIENTS ?? '5', 10);
19	const OPS_PER_CLIENT = 45;
20	
21	describe('Mixed Workload', () => {
22	  let pool: PoolClient[] = [];
23	
24	  afterAll(async () => {
25	    await destroyClientPool(pool);
26	  });
27	
28	  it(`${NUM_CLIENTS} clients × ${OPS_PER_CLIENT} mixed ops`, async () => {
29	    pool = await createClientPool({
30	      clientCount: NUM_CLIENTS,
31	      label: 'mixed',
32	    });
33	
34	    const results = await Promise.allSettled(
35	      pool.map((pc) => {
36	        pc.metrics.start();
37	        return runMixedWorkload(pc, {
38	          totalOps: OPS_PER_CLIENT,
39	          weights: {
40	            createFolder: 2,
41	            uploadFile: 4,
42	            renameItem: 1,
43	            moveItem: 1,
44	            deleteItem: 1,
45	          },
46	        }).finally(() => pc.metrics.stop());
47	      })
48	    );
49	
50	    const succeeded = results.filter((r) => r.status === 'fulfilled').length;
51	    console.log(`\nClients completed: ${succeeded}/${pool.length}`);
52	
53	    const metrics = await aggregateAndReport('Mixed Workload', pool);
54	
55	    // Threshold check: generous limits for mixed workload (higher error rate historically)
56	    const THRESHOLDS: ThresholdConfig[] = [
57	      { operation: 'uploadFile', p95MaxMs: 10_000, errorRateMax: 0.1 },
58	      { operation: 'createFolder', p95MaxMs: 5_000, errorRateMax: 0.1 },
59	    ];
60	
61	    expectThresholdsPassed(metrics, THRESHOLDS);
62	  });
63	});
64	
```

> AGENT

Got the numbers. **That "failure" is a 34 ms overshoot on an arbitrary threshold with zero errors — not a regression signal.**

Mixed-workload @ 50 clients (2,177 ops, **0 errors**):

| Op | count | p50 | p95 | p99 | throughput |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| createFolder | 541 | 3,131ms | **5,034ms** ⟵ trips 5,000 gate | 5,591ms | 3.32/s |
| uploadFile | 1,003 | 4,147ms | 5,857ms | 6,767ms | 6.15/s |
| **moveItem** | 207 | 6,523ms | **9,142ms** | 10,393ms | 1.27/s |
| renameItem | 205 | 1,372ms | 3,074ms | 3,634ms | 1.26/s |
| deleteItem | 221 | 1,344ms | 3,133ms | 3,633ms | 1.35/s |

**Root cause:** createFolder p95 came in at 5,034ms vs the hardcoded 5,000ms gate — **0.7% over**, zero errors, zero timeouts. createFolder is IPNS-publish-bound, and at 50 concurrent clients on the 4-vCPU staging VPS that publish queue pushes p95 right to ~5s. The March anchor confirms the scaling curve: createFolder p95 was 11.9s at 200 clients, so ~5s at 50 is exactly on-trend. The gate is mis-calibrated for 50-client staging, not the API regressing. There's also no prior mixed-workload@50 staging baseline to regress *against* — this run **is** the new baseline for that config, and it's healthy.

**The actual finding worth your attention:** `moveItem` is the real tail hotspot — p95 9.1s, p99 10.4s, slower than uploadFile. That's structural (a move = two folder-metadata updates + two IPNS publishes), not necessarily a regression, but it's where the latency lives and it's currently ungated.

Two things I'll tee up for the re-baselining (not touching code mid-sweep):
1. The createFolder 5s gate should be concurrency-scaled (or relaxed for 50+ clients) — it's failing green runs on a knife-edge.
2. Add a `moveItem` threshold so the slowest real operation is actually tracked.

Sweep is proceeding normally — run 2 (`mixed-workload` @ 100) in progress. I'll hold for the full set and deliver the consolidated diff + baseline-refresh proposal at the end.

All 7 done. Here's the verdict, and it confirms your instinct — **there's a large, consistent upload-path regression vs the last recorded staging baseline.**

## Headline: upload throughput ~halved, latency ~doubled

`upload-throughput` is the clean apples-to-apples anchor (same workflow, same VPS, same scenario as the Phase 19.2 post-deploy staging baseline):

| Clients | Metric | 19.2 baseline | **Now (06-22)** | Δ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 50 | throughput | 15.10/s | **7.71/s** | **−49%** |
| 50 | p50 / p95 | 3,242 / 4,615ms | **6,364 / 8,760ms** | **+96% / +90%** |
| 100 | throughput | 18.76/s | **9.01/s** | **−52%** |
| 100 | p50 / p95 | 5,300 / 8,300ms | **11,112 / 15,050ms** | **+110% / +81%** |
| 200 | throughput | 19.22/s | **9.54/s** | **−50%** |
| 200 | p50 / p95 | 10,500 / 15,500ms | **20,782 / 31,034ms** | **+98% / +100%** |

Error rates are basically unchanged (0% at 50/100, 1.6% at 200) — **the system isn't breaking, it's just ~2× slower.** That consistency across all three client counts rules out noise/contention; it's a real pipeline regression.

**`sustained-load` @ 200 confirms it** (vs March `staging-sustained-load.json`): ~33% fewer ops completed in the same 5-min window (11,174 → 7,479), uploadFile p95 +50% (11.6s → 17.5s), and the **p99 tail blew up** — uploadFile p99 13.2s → **55.6s** (+321%), createFolder p99 15.7s → 40.3s.

**`mixed-workload`** has no prior staging anchor (the only earlier run was 5-client local + 100% errored), so it's a fresh baseline — healthy error-wise (0/1/16 errors at 50/100/200) but it surfaced one pathology: **`moveItem` degrades super-linearly** — p95 9.1s → **42.3s** going from 50→100 clients. A move = two folder-metadata updates + two IPNS publishes, and that doubles down hard under contention. It's currently ungated.

## Most likely root cause — check this first

The magnitude is the tell: **~2× slower upload almost exactly matches the documented Phase 19.2 flatfs→pebbleds delta.** The strongest hypothesis is that **staging's Kubo datastore is no longer on the `pebbleds` profile** — a redeploy that reset `IPFS_PROFILE` to the default `flatfs` would reproduce this regression on its own, with zero code changes. That's the single highest-probability cause given how cleanly the numbers line up.

To confirm and split infra-vs-code, I'd:
1. **Check staging Kubo datastore** — `ipfs config Datastore.Spec.type` on the staging VPS (should be `pebbleds`; if it's `flatfs`, that's almost certainly the whole story).
2. **Pull staging Prometheus histograms** (`cipherbox_ipfs_*_duration_seconds`) — if server-side pin/publish latency doubled, it's infra; if it's flat, the added latency is in the client-side SDK path (e.g. the newer `selectEncryptionMode`/pipeline changes running on the runner).

## Next steps — pick one

The full per-op metrics are saved under `loadtest-staging-2026-06-22/` (scratch, uncommitted).

Want me to:
- **(a) Investigate the root cause** — SSH to staging (1Password agent) to check the datastore profile + pull `/metrics`, and report back whether this is infra or code? *(recommended — cheap, and likely conclusive)*
- **(b) Persist these as the fresh staging baselines** — write the 7 runs into `tests/load/baselines/` + a dated `.planning/baselines/` doc so the next round has a current anchor?
- **(c) Fix the threshold calibration** — concurrency-scale the createFolder gate and add a `moveItem` gate so green runs stop failing on knife-edges and the real hotspot gets tracked?

My call: **(a) first** — no point re-baselining a number that's 2× off if a one-line `IPFS_PROFILE` fix restores it.

```
225	      contents: write
226	    steps:
227	      - uses: actions/checkout@9c091bb21b7c1c1d1991bb908d89e4e9dddfe3e0 # v7.0.0
228	        with:
229	          ref: ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}
230	
231	      - name: Install WinFsp
232	        shell: powershell
233	        run: |
234	          $url = "https://github.com/winfsp/winfsp/releases/download/v2.1/winfsp-2.1.25156.msi"
235	          # Use Join-Path to produce proper Windows backslash paths —
236	          # msiexec.exe treats '/' as a flag prefix, so forward slashes in
237	          # the file path cause "package could not be opened" errors.
238	          $resourceDir = Join-Path "${{ github.workspace }}" "apps\desktop\src-tauri\resources"
239	          $out = Join-Path $resourceDir "winfsp-2.1.25156.msi"
240	          $expectedHash = "073A70E00F77423E34BED98B86E600DEF93393BA5822204FAC57A29324DB9F7A"
241	          New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $resourceDir
242	          Invoke-WebRequest -Uri $url -OutFile $out
243	          $actualHash = (Get-FileHash $out -Algorithm SHA256).Hash
244	          if ($actualHash -ne $expectedHash) { throw "WinFsp MSI hash mismatch: expected $expectedHash, got $actualHash" }
245	          $proc = Start-Process msiexec.exe -ArgumentList "/i","`"$out`"","/qn","INSTALLLEVEL=1000" -Wait -NoNewWindow -PassThru
246	          if ($proc.ExitCode -ne 0) { throw "msiexec failed with exit code $($proc.ExitCode)" }
247	          # winfsp-sys build.rs reads HKLM\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\WinFsp\InstallDir
248	          # to find headers/libs. The MSI doesn't reliably write this key on CI runners.
249	          $installDir = "C:\Program Files (x86)\WinFsp"
250	          if (!(Test-Path "HKLM:\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\WinFsp")) {
251	            New-Item -Path "HKLM:\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\WinFsp" -Force | Out-Null
252	          }
253	          New-ItemProperty -Path "HKLM:\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\WinFsp" -Name "InstallDir" -Value $installDir -PropertyType String -Force | Out-Null
254	          # Verify registry key was written correctly
255	          $regVal = Get-ItemProperty -Path "HKLM:\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\WinFsp" -Name "InstallDir" -ErrorAction Stop
256	          if (-not $regVal.InstallDir -or $regVal.InstallDir -ne $installDir) {
257	            throw "WinFsp InstallDir registry mismatch: expected '$installDir', got '$($regVal.InstallDir)'"
258	          }
259	          Write-Host "WinFsp InstallDir registry: $($regVal.InstallDir)"
260	          if (!(Test-Path "$installDir\lib")) { throw "WinFsp lib directory not found at $installDir\lib" }
261	          if (!(Test-Path "$installDir\inc")) { throw "WinFsp inc directory not found at $installDir\inc" }

384	          projectPath: apps/desktop
385	          tauriScript: pnpm tauri
386	          tagName: ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}
387	          releaseName: 'CipherBox Desktop ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}'
388	          releaseBody: 'Staging build for ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}. macOS (unsigned - right-click > Open to launch), Windows (unsigned - click "More info" > "Run anyway"), and Linux (AppImage/deb).'
389	          releaseDraft: false
390	          prerelease: false
391	          includeUpdaterJson: true
392	          args: -- --no-default-features --features fuse

394	  deploy-vps:
395	    name: Deploy to Staging VPS
396	    needs: [build-api, build-tee, build-web]
397	    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
398	    environment: staging
399	    permissions:
400	      contents: read
401	      packages: read
402	    steps:
403	      - uses: actions/checkout@9c091bb21b7c1c1d1991bb908d89e4e9dddfe3e0 # v7.0.0
404	        with:
405	          ref: ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}
406	
407	      - name: Download web dist artifact
408	        uses: actions/download-artifact@3e5f45b2cfb9172054b4087a40e8e0b5a5461e7c # v8.0.1
409	        with:
410	          name: web-dist
411	          path: web-dist/
412	
413	      - name: Generate .env.staging
414	        run: |
415	          cat > .env.staging << 'ENVEOF'
416	          NODE_ENV=staging
417	          CIPHERBOX_ENVIRONMENT=staging
418	          TEST_LOGIN_SECRET=${{ secrets.STAGING_TEST_LOGIN_SECRET }}
419	          PORT=3000
420	          DB_HOST=postgres
421	          DB_PORT=5432
422	          DB_USERNAME=${{ vars.STAGING_DB_USERNAME }}
423	          DB_PASSWORD=REDACTED secrets.STAGING_DB_PASSWORD }}
424	          DB_DATABASE=cipherbox_staging
425	          JWT_SECRET=${{ secrets.STAGING_JWT_SECRET }}
426	          CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS=${{ vars.CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS }}
427	          IPFS_PROVIDER=local
428	          IPFS_LOCAL_API_URL=http://ipfs:5001
429	          IPFS_LOCAL_GATEWAY_URL=http://ipfs:8080
430	          DELEGATED_ROUTING_URL=http://someguy:8190
431	          DELEGATED_ROUTING_FALLBACK_URL=https://delegated-ipfs.dev
432	          THROTTLE_BYPASS_SECRET=${{ secrets.STAGING_THROTTLE_BYPASS_SECRET }}
433	          REDIS_HOST=redis
434	          REDIS_PORT=6379
435	          TEE_WORKER_URL=http://tee-worker:3001
436	          TEE_WORKER_SECRET=${{ secrets.STAGING_TEE_WORKER_SECRET }}
437	          REDIS_PASSWORD=REDACTED secrets.STAGING_REDIS_PASSWORD }}
438	          GRAFANA_LOKI_URL=${{ vars.GRAFANA_LOKI_URL }}
439	          GRAFANA_LOKI_USERNAME=${{ vars.GRAFANA_LOKI_USERNAME }}
440	          GRAFANA_LOKI_API_KEY=${{ secrets.GRAFANA_LOKI_API_KEY }}
441	          GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_URL=${{ vars.GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_URL }}
442	          GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_USERNAME=${{ vars.GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_USERNAME }}
443	          GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_API_KEY=${{ secrets.GRAFANA_PROMETHEUS_API_KEY }}
444	          SENDGRID_API_KEY=${{ secrets.SENDGRID_API_KEY }}
445	          SENDGRID_FROM_EMAIL=${{ vars.SENDGRID_FROM_EMAIL }}
446	          GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID=${{ vars.GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID }}
447	          IDENTITY_JWT_PRIVATE_KEY=${{ secrets.IDENTITY_JWT_PRIVATE_KEY }}
448	          ENVEOF
449	          # Append image tag, lowercase owner, and component versions (can't use shell vars in quoted heredoc)
450	          OWNER="${{ github.repository_owner }}"
451	          echo "GITHUB_REPOSITORY_OWNER=${OWNER,,}" >> .env.staging
452	          echo "TAG=${{ env.DEPLOY_TAG }}" >> .env.staging
453	          API_VER=$(node -e "console.log(require('./apps/api/package.json').version)")
454	          TEE_VER=$(node -e "console.log(require('./apps/tee-worker/package.json').version)")
455	          echo "API_VERSION=${API_VER}" >> .env.staging
456	          echo "TEE_VERSION=${TEE_VER}" >> .env.staging
457	
458	      - name: Copy files to VPS
459	        uses: appleboy/scp-action@ff85246acaad7bdce478db94a363cd2bf7c90345 # v1.0.0
460	        with:
461	          host: ${{ vars.STAGING_HOST }}
462	          username: ${{ vars.STAGING_USER }}
463	          key: ${{ secrets.STAGING_SSH_KEY }}
464	          source: '.env.staging,docker/docker-compose.staging.yml,docker/Caddyfile,docker/alloy-config.river'
465	          target: /opt/cipherbox
466	          strip_components: 0
467	
468	      - name: Arrange files on VPS
469	        uses: appleboy/ssh-action@0ff4204d59e8e51228ff73bce53f80d53301dee2 # v1.2.5
470	        with:
471	          host: ${{ vars.STAGING_HOST }}
472	          username: ${{ vars.STAGING_USER }}
473	          key: ${{ secrets.STAGING_SSH_KEY }}
474	          script: |
475	            # Move files to correct locations
476	            mv /opt/cipherbox/.env.staging /opt/cipherbox/docker/.env.staging 2>/dev/null || true
477	            cd /opt/cipherbox/docker
478	
479	            # Docker Compose reads .env automatically for variable substitution
480	            cp .env.staging .env
481	
482	            # Restrict env file permissions (contains secrets)
483	            chmod 600 .env .env.staging
484	
485	            # Ensure certs symlink exists for Caddy
486	            rm -rf /opt/cipherbox/docker/certs
487	            ln -sf /opt/cipherbox/certs /opt/cipherbox/docker/certs
488	
489	      - name: Deploy web app files to VPS
490	        uses: appleboy/scp-action@ff85246acaad7bdce478db94a363cd2bf7c90345 # v1.0.0
491	        with:
492	          host: ${{ vars.STAGING_HOST }}
493	          username: ${{ vars.STAGING_USER }}
494	          key: ${{ secrets.STAGING_SSH_KEY }}
495	          source: 'web-dist/*'
496	          target: /opt/cipherbox/web
497	          strip_components: 1
498	          rm: true
499	
500	      - name: Deploy services
501	        uses: appleboy/ssh-action@0ff4204d59e8e51228ff73bce53f80d53301dee2 # v1.2.5
502	        with:
503	          host: ${{ vars.STAGING_HOST }}
504	          username: ${{ vars.STAGING_USER }}
505	          key: ${{ secrets.STAGING_SSH_KEY }}
506	          script: |
507	            set -euo pipefail
508	            cd /opt/cipherbox/docker
509	
510	            # Login to GHCR
511	            echo "${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}" | docker login ghcr.io -u ${{ github.actor }} --password-stdin
512	
513	            # Set image tag from git tag (GHCR requires lowercase owner)
514	            export TAG="${{ env.DEPLOY_TAG }}"
515	            OWNER="${{ github.repository_owner }}"
516	            export GITHUB_REPOSITORY_OWNER="${OWNER,,}"
517	
518	            # Pull new images
519	            docker compose -f docker-compose.staging.yml pull
520	
521	            # Run database migrations before starting services
522	            # (api service inherits env vars from env_file in compose)
523	            docker compose -f docker-compose.staging.yml run --rm \
524	              api node dist/run-migrations.js
525	
526	            # Start/restart services
527	            docker compose -f docker-compose.staging.yml up -d
528	
529	            # Restart Caddy to pick up refreshed web files bind mount
530	            # (scp rm:true recreates the host dir, breaking the old mount inode)
531	            docker compose -f docker-compose.staging.yml restart caddy
532	
533	            # Cleanup old images
534	            docker image prune -f
535	
536	  provision-dashboard:
537	    name: Provision Grafana Dashboard
538	    needs: [deploy-vps]
539	    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
540	    environment: staging
541	    permissions:
542	      contents: read
543	    steps:
544	      - uses: actions/checkout@9c091bb21b7c1c1d1991bb908d89e4e9dddfe3e0 # v7.0.0
545	        with:
546	          ref: ${{ inputs.staging_tag || github.ref_name }}
547	
548	      - name: Wait for Grafana Cloud readiness
549	        env:
550	          GRAFANA_URL: ${{ vars.GRAFANA_URL }}
551	          GRAFANA_API_KEY: ${{ secrets.GRAFANA_API_KEY }}
552	        run: |
553	          set -euo pipefail
554	          : "${GRAFANA_URL:?GRAFANA_URL is required for Grafana readiness check}"
555	          : "${GRAFANA_API_KEY:?GRAFANA_API_KEY is required for Grafana readiness check}"
556	          GRAFANA_URL="${GRAFANA_URL%/}"
557	          MAX_ATTEMPTS=30
558	          INTERVAL=10
559	          for ((i=1; i<=MAX_ATTEMPTS; i++)); do
560	            HTTP_CODE=$(curl -sS --connect-timeout 10 --max-time 15 \
561	              -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" \
562	              -H "Authorization: Bearer ${GRAFANA_API_KEY}" \
563	              "${GRAFANA_URL}/api/health") || HTTP_CODE=000
564	            if [[ "$HTTP_CODE" == "200" ]]; then
565	              echo "Grafana Cloud is ready (attempt $i/$MAX_ATTEMPTS)"
566	              exit 0
567	            elif [[ "$HTTP_CODE" =~ ^(401|403|404)$ ]]; then
568	              echo "::error::Grafana returned HTTP $HTTP_CODE — check GRAFANA_URL and GRAFANA_API_KEY"
569	              exit 1
570	            fi
571	            echo "Grafana not ready (HTTP $HTTP_CODE), waiting ${INTERVAL}s... ($i/$MAX_ATTEMPTS)"
572	            sleep "$INTERVAL"
573	          done
574	          echo "::error::Grafana Cloud did not become ready after $((MAX_ATTEMPTS * INTERVAL))s"
575	          exit 1
576	
577	      - name: Push dashboard to Grafana Cloud
578	        env:
579	          GRAFANA_URL: ${{ vars.GRAFANA_URL }}
580	          GRAFANA_API_KEY: ${{ secrets.GRAFANA_API_KEY }}
581	          DS_METRICS_UID: ${{ vars.GRAFANA_DS_METRICS_UID }}
582	          DS_LOGS_UID: ${{ vars.GRAFANA_DS_LOGS_UID }}
583	        run: |
584	          set -euo pipefail
585	          : "${GRAFANA_URL:?GRAFANA_URL is required}"
586	          : "${GRAFANA_API_KEY:?GRAFANA_API_KEY is required}"
587	          : "${DS_METRICS_UID:?GRAFANA_DS_METRICS_UID is required}"
588	          : "${DS_LOGS_UID:?GRAFANA_DS_LOGS_UID is required}"
589	          GRAFANA_URL="${GRAFANA_URL%/}"
590	
591	          # The dashboard JSON uses __inputs for datasource templating.
592	          # Replace variable references with actual datasource UIDs.
593	          DASHBOARD=$(cat docker/grafana/dashboards/cipherbox-staging.json \
594	            | jq --arg metrics "$DS_METRICS_UID" --arg logs "$DS_LOGS_UID" \
595	                 'del(.__inputs)
596	                  | .id = null
597	                  | walk(if type == "object" and .uid? and (.uid | test("\\$\\{DS_")) then
598	                      .uid = (if .uid == "${DS_GRAFANA_CLOUD_METRICS}" then $metrics
599	                              elif .uid == "${DS_GRAFANA_CLOUD_LOGS}" then $logs
600	                              else .uid end)
601	                    else . end)')
602	
603	          # Fail if any datasource placeholder survived the rewrite
604	          if jq -e '.. | objects | .uid? | select(type == "string" and test("^\\$\\{DS_"))' \
605	            >/dev/null <<<"$DASHBOARD"; then
606	            echo "::error::Unresolved datasource placeholder(s) remain in dashboard JSON"
607	            exit 1
608	          fi
609	
610	          # Wrap in import payload
611	          PAYLOAD=$(jq -n --argjson dashboard "$DASHBOARD" '{
612	            dashboard: $dashboard,
613	            overwrite: true,
614	            message: "Provisioned by deploy-staging workflow"
615	          }')
616	
617	          # Push to Grafana API
618	          HTTP_CODE=$(curl -sS --connect-timeout 10 --max-time 60 \
619	            -o /tmp/grafana-response.json -w "%{http_code}" \
620	            -X POST "${GRAFANA_URL}/api/dashboards/db" \
621	            -H "Authorization: Bearer ${GRAFANA_API_KEY}" \
622	            -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
623	            -d "$PAYLOAD")
624	
625	          if [[ ! "$HTTP_CODE" =~ ^2[0-9][0-9]$ ]]; then
626	            echo "::error::Grafana API returned HTTP $HTTP_CODE"
627	            cat /tmp/grafana-response.json
628	            exit 1
629	          fi
630	
631	          echo "Dashboard provisioned successfully (HTTP $HTTP_CODE)"
632	          jq -r '.url // "unknown"' /tmp/grafana-response.json
633	
```

> AGENT

The compose file is revealing — two infra-level suspects jump out, and one of them is more likely than my datastore hypothesis:

1. **The `ipfs` (Kubo) container is CPU-capped at `1.0` core** (and someguy at another `1.0`), on a 4-vCPU box (lines 84-88). Kubo is the upload/IPNS bottleneck — pinning it to a single core would directly halve throughput under concurrency.
2. **`IPFS_PROFILE: server,pebbleds` is declared, but the comment (lines 66-68) warns it only applies to a *fresh* repo** — if the `ipfs_staging` volume wasn't wiped, the on-disk datastore could still be flatfs regardless of this env.

Both are config, not code. Let me check git history for when these were introduced (relative to the March 19.2 baseline) and verify the 1Password SSH agent before connecting.

> AGENT

The git history **refutes both of my infra hypotheses** — important to be precise here:

- **Kubo's 1-CPU cap is not new.** `cpus: '1.0'` traces to `0e9cb1e0e` (2026-03-23), *before* the 19.2 pebbleds baseline (`8f8f03fa6`, 03-24). The good baseline was captured *with* Kubo already pinned to one core — so the cap is a real bottleneck but it's a constant, not the regression.
- **pebbleds is still declared and unchanged** since 03-24; the only staging-compose change since is the May TEE-worker swap (#472), which doesn't touch the upload path.

So the infra config hasn't meaningfully changed since the good baseline — which points the finger at **deployed API/SDK code or accumulated data/state**, not compose config. The decisive test is the server-side Prometheus histograms: if Kubo pin/publish latency doubled, it's infra/data-bloat; if it's flat, the regression is client/SDK-side.

The agent has **"Hostinger Cipherbox VPS Root"** loaded. SSHing in now to capture runtime state in one session (datastore type, repo size, container stats, deployed API version, and server-side latency means). You'll get a Touch ID prompt.

> AGENT

Major data point: the box is **2 vCPU / 7.8 GiB** now — but it's been up **134 days** (no reboot), and the 19.2 baseline (Mar 23–24) falls inside that same uptime window. So the CPU count is *also* a constant, not a change (the Phase 18 doc's "4 vCPU" was stale). Net: **every infra dimension — CPU count, Kubo CPU cap, datastore profile, Kubo version — was identical at the good baseline.** What *has* changed massively: **API `0.24.2` → `0.43.0`** (~19 minor versions) and the **IPFS repo ballooned ~320 MiB → 5.9 GB**.

The output got truncated before the decisive metrics. Re-running just the latency histograms + datastore + DB size (one more Touch ID), with per-command timeouts so nothing hangs.

> AGENT

The decisive metric came through — and it pins the root cause. Computing the means (these counters are dominated by my just-run sweep, since the API container is only 2h old):

- **Kubo `pin` mean latency: 226,787s / 75,181 = `3.02s`** — vs the **19.2 baseline of `1.37s`**. **+120% (2.2× slower).**

That doubling is **server-side, in Kubo** — which fully accounts for the ~2× upload regression. So it is **not** client/SDK code and **not** compose config (all infra dims are unchanged). The one thing that *did* change: the **IPFS repo grew ~320 MiB → 5.9 GB (~18×)**, saturating the resource-capped Kubo (1 CPU / 2 GB, currently at 71% mem). Three months of load-test churn piled up blocks that were never garbage-collected.

(My `timeout dc` wrapper failed because `dc` is a shell function — re-running datastore confirmation + repo/DB accumulation stats directly.)

`</dev/null` was the missing piece — `docker compose exec -T` drains the heredoc from stdin, swallowing the rest of the script (which is why each session cut off right after the first `exec`). But I got what I needed there: **datastore `type: pebbleds`, format v24 — pebbleds is genuinely active on disk** (flatfs hypothesis fully refuted). > AGENT

Root cause confirmed — and it's **operational data bloat, not a code regression.**

## The evidence chain

| Signal | 19.2 baseline | Now | |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kubo `pin` mean (server-side)** | 1.37s | **3.02s** | **+120%** |
| POST `/ipfs/upload` mean | — | 3.22s | of which **3.02s is the Kubo pin** → API overhead ~0.2s |
| IPFS repo size | ~320 MiB | **6.2 GB** | 294,811 objects |
| Actually pinned / live | — | **17,875 CIDs / ~489 MB** | `pinned_cids` table agrees (17,867 rows) |

The upload endpoint spends **3.0 of its 3.2 seconds inside the Kubo pin call** — so the regression is squarely in Kubo, not API/SDK code (the API jumped 0.24→0.43 but adds only ~0.2s of overhead). And the smoking gun: **the repo is 6.2 GB / 294k objects, but only ~489 MB / 17,875 CIDs are pinned/live — ~90% of the datastore is unpinned garbage** from three months of load-test churn (create→delete leaves orphaned blocks until GC). That 6.2 GB pebbleds store vastly exceeds Kubo's 2 GB memory cap (currently 71% used), so pins hit disk on the write path → latency doubles → throughput halves.

Everything else is unchanged from the good baseline, which is why it *isn't* the cause: **2 vCPU** (134-day uptime spans the March baseline), **Kubo CPU cap 1.0** (since 03-23), **datastore confirmed `pebbleds`** (type/format v24 on disk), **Kubo v0.40.0**. The compose config and box are constants; only accumulated state changed.

## The fix (cheap, low-risk, high-confidence)

1. **`ipfs repo gc`** on staging — reclaims the ~5.7 GB of unpinned garbage. GC only removes *unpinned* blocks, so live files are untouched. Expect repo 6.2 GB → ~0.5–1 GB.
2. **Re-run `upload-throughput`** @ 50/100/200 — I expect pin to drop back toward ~1.4s and throughput toward ~15/18/19 ops/s. That both confirms the diagnosis and tells you whether *any* residual regression is real code.
3. **Longer-term:** enable Kubo's periodic GC (daemon `--enable-gc` / `Datastore.GCPeriod`) or a cron so this can't silently recur; consider bumping Kubo's mem cap (2 G → 3–4 G — there's headroom, host is at 2.8/7.8 GiB). Also a methodology note: **the load test itself generates this garbage**, so future baseline runs should GC first, or the harness should clean up its test accounts' content (it deletes accounts but the blocks linger until GC).

## Want me to proceed?

This is where I stop and check — running GC mutates your staging instance, and you're mid-deployment. My recommendation:

- **(a) Run `ipfs repo gc` now + re-run `upload-throughput` @ 50/100/200** to confirm recovery — ~20 min, gives you a clean before/after. *(recommended)*
- **(b) Just GC, no re-run** — fastest path to restore staging perf.
- **(c) Hold** — I've given you the diagnosis; you handle the GC on your own schedule.

I'd also still like to **(d) persist these 7 runs as fresh `tests/load/baselines/` + a dated `.planning/baselines/` doc** regardless — but I'd run that *after* a post-GC re-measure so the recorded baseline reflects a clean datastore, not the bloated one. Which do you want?

```
93	  private async runDriftReport(): Promise<void> {
94	    // D-06: Read-only diff — never deletes anything
95	    let kuboPins: Set<string>;
96	    try {
97	      kuboPins = await this.fetchKuboPins();
98	    } catch (err) {
99	      const message = err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
100	      this.logger.warn(`Drift report: Kubo pin/ls failed, skipping run: ${message}`);
101	      return;
102	    }
103	
104	    // Build DB accounted set: pinned_cids ∪ pending_unpins
105	    // IN-05 disposition: dbCids intentionally includes ALL pinned_cids rows — including
106	    // BYO advisory rows — to mirror the guardedUnpin refcount semantics (WR-07 accepted:
107	    // BYO advisory rows block physical unpin, so they must also be counted as "accounted"
108	    // by the drift report; filtering them out would make the report report false orphans
109	    // for CIDs that are intentionally retained by a BYO advisory row).
110	    const [pinnedCidRows, pendingUnpinRows] = await Promise.all([
111	      this.pinnedCidRepository.find({ select: { cid: true } }),
112	      this.pendingUnpinRepository.find({ select: { cid: true } }),
113	    ]);
114	
115	    const dbCids = new Set<string>([
116	      ...pinnedCidRows.map((r) => r.cid),
117	      ...pendingUnpinRows.map((r) => r.cid),
118	    ]);
119	
120	    // Report pins that Kubo knows about but our DB does not
121	    for (const cid of kuboPins) {
122	      if (!dbCids.has(cid)) {
123	        // WR-04 disposition: Counter is intentional here, not a Gauge. Each drift run
124	        // appends orphan events to a cumulative total so operators can track how many
125	        // orphan detections have occurred since the process started. A Gauge would
126	        // require resetting between runs and tracking ephemeral per-run state; cumulative
127	        // counts are more useful for alerting and trend analysis. (WR-04 accepted per
128	        // 42-REVIEW author judgement.)
129	        this.metricsService.driftOrphanedPinsTotal.inc();
130	        this.logger.warn(`Drift: unaccounted Kubo pin cid=${cid}`);
131	      }
132	    }
133	  }
134	
135	  /**
136	   * Fetch all recursive pins from Kubo using NDJSON line-by-line parsing.
137	   * Kubo pin/ls returns NDJSON (not a single JSON object) — see Pitfall 6.
138	   */
139	  private async fetchKuboPins(): Promise<Set<string>> {
140	    let response: Response;
141	    try {
142	      response = await fetch(`${this.apiUrl}/api/v0/pin/ls?type=recursive`, {
143	        method: 'POST',
144	        signal: AbortSignal.timeout(KUBO_PIN_LS_TIMEOUT_MS),
145	      });
146	    } catch (err) {
147	      // AbortSignal.timeout fires a TimeoutError; surface a clear message so the
148	      // drift job's failure is diagnosable rather than an opaque hang/abort.
149	      const message = err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
150	      throw new Error(
151	        `Kubo pin/ls request failed or timed out after ${KUBO_PIN_LS_TIMEOUT_MS}ms: ${message}`
152	      );
153	    }
154	
155	    if (!response.ok) {
156	      const text = await response.text();
157	      throw new Error(`Kubo pin/ls failed: ${response.status} ${text}`);
158	    }
159	
160	    const text = await response.text();
161	    const pins = new Set<string>();
162	
163	    for (const line of text.split('\n')) {
164	      const trimmed = line.trim();
165	      if (!trimmed) continue;
166	      try {
167	        const obj = JSON.parse(trimmed) as { Keys?: Record<string, unknown> };
168	        if (obj.Keys) {
169	          for (const cid of Object.keys(obj.Keys)) {
170	            pins.add(cid);
171	          }
172	        }
173	      } catch {
174	        // Malformed line — skip and continue (T-42-21 mitigation)
175	        this.logger.warn(`Drift report: failed to parse Kubo pin/ls line: ${trimmed}`);
176	      }
177	    }
178	
179	    return pins;
180	  }
181	}
```

> AGENT

Pulled the actual logic (`pending-unpin.processor.ts:93` `runDriftReport()`) — and there are two corrections to make, one of which matters a lot.

**1. The value is 39, not 32.**

**2. It's a cumulative `Counter`, not a gauge — so 39 is *not* "items currently orphaned."** The code (`metrics.service.ts:184` `new client.Counter`) `.inc()`s once per orphaned CID *on every hourly drift run* and never resets (except on container restart). The comment at line 123 spells this out: it's an append-only count of orphan *detections* over time, for trend/alerting. Since the API container was only ~2h old at capture, 39 = the orphans summed across however many hourly runs fired in that window — the same persistently-orphaned CID gets re-counted each run. To read it as a live count you'd have to divide by the number of runs, which you can't do from the counter alone.

**3. The definition is slightly broader than you said.** An "orphan" is a **recursive** Kubo pin whose CID is in **neither `pinned_cids` *nor* `pending_unpins`**:

```
orphan = (cid ∈ kubo `pin ls --type=recursive`)  AND  (cid ∉ pinned_cids ∪ pending_unpins)
```

So it excludes pending_unpins (CIDs already queued for removal aren't flagged), and only looks at recursive pins (indirect/child pins are ignored). Raw-string CID compare — same as my set-diff below would be.

**What the *actual current* count looks like:** from the pre-GC snapshot — Kubo recursive pins **17,875**, `pinned_cids` **17,867**, `pending_unpins` **0** — the net gap is only **8** (orphaned − missing = 8). So the true live drift is single/low-double digits, well under 0.05% of pins — not hundreds. GC doesn't touch pinned blocks, so that's still ~current.

If you want the exact distinct orphan count (`|kubo_recursive \ (pinned_cids ∪ pending_unpins)|`) plus the reverse "missing" set, I can compute it directly with a set-diff on staging — but I'd run it **after the recheck finishes**, since the recheck is actively creating/deleting pins right now and would pollute the snapshot with transient mid-flight discrepancies. Want me to grab that clean number once it's done?

> AGENT

Here's the full picture — and it includes a **correction to my earlier confident diagnosis.**

## Orphan question, answered cleanly (post-recheck, no churn)

- **Orphaned (Kubo-pinned, not in DB): 39** · **Missing (in DB, not pinned on Kubo): 31**
- Reconciles exactly: 17,875 Kubo recursive pins − 17,867 DB cids = net **8** = 39 − 31. ✓
- Both are tiny (<0.25% of pins) — normal crash/race drift; the report is read-only (never deletes). And the counter reading `39` happens to equal the live count because the hourly drift job has run exactly once since the 2h-old container started. The 39 orphaned are a minor block leak; the 31 missing are DB rows whose files Kubo can't serve.

## Recheck verdict: GC did **not** restore throughput — my bloat hypothesis is largely refuted

Server-side pin latency, isolated to just the recheck's 21,184 pins:

| | 19.2 baseline | pre-GC sweep | **post-GC recheck** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kubo pin mean | **1.37s** | 3.02s | **4.19s** |
| ipns-publish mean | ~0.05–0.83s | 0.83s | **1.42s** |

The repo is now **69k objects / 3.2 GB** (was 294k / 6.2 GB) — ~4× fewer objects — yet pin latency is **no better** (arguably worse). So **block-garbage bloat was not the primary cause.** I was wrong to lead with that as the fix; GC is still worth doing as hygiene (#547), but it doesn't explain the 2× regression.

## Honest caveat before re-diagnosing

The recheck is **confounded**: it ran (a) immediately after GC while pebbleds was still compacting 274k deletions, and (b) after ~2 hours of continuous heavy load from my sweeps. Both inflate pin latency. So I can't cleanly separate "GC didn't help" from "measured during compaction + load fatigue."

What's *not* confounded: pin latency is **~3–4× the March baseline on identical infra**, so a real server-side regression exists. Leading suspects now (unconfirmed):

1. **Accumulated pinset size** — 17,875 persistent pins (vs ~hundreds in March) inflating Kubo pin/reprovide cost on the 1-CPU cap. GC-invariant, which fits "GC didn't help."
2. **API/SDK code** (0.24→0.43) adding upload-path work.

## Decision point — how deep do you want to go?

I've gone well past "configs," so your call:

- **(a) Nail it (recommended):** let staging idle ~30–45 min (compaction + reprovide settle), then I run *one* clean `upload-throughput@50` for a steady-state number, plus a single-`pin`-at-idle test (isolates per-pin cost vs concurrency-queuing). That disambiguates pinset/reprovide vs code in ~one pass.
- **(b) Capture & pause:** I append this regression finding to #547 (or open a dedicated perf-regression issue) and you pick it up with the team.
- **(c) Re-baseline as-is** and accept the current numbers.

I'd do (a) — but after an idle window, not now, so the measurement is clean.

> DEVELOPER

ok, so likely need to run a manual GC on kubo

would restarting the kubo docker container possibly help?

> AGENT

Possibly — but with caveats, and it's easy to fool yourself with it.

**What a Kubo restart actually does:** clears all in-memory state — goroutines, the connection manager, the DHT routing table, the **reprovider queue**, and accumulated heap pressure (Kubo was at 1.55/2 GiB = 77% of its cap after 2h of load). It re-opens pebbleds with a **cold block cache**. No data loss — the volume persists, all 17,875 pins survive. Cost: ~30s where the API can't pin/upload.

**Why it might help:** if the regression is partly *transient runtime degradation* — heap near the cap forcing aggressive Go GC, a backed-up reprovider, connection churn, or pebbleds state right after the GC's 274k-delete compaction — a fresh daemon relieves all of that. Given I hammered staging for ~2h straight, some of the slowdown is plausibly this.

**Why it's a measurement trap:** a fresh daemon starts with an **empty reprovide queue and cold caches**, so you get a **honeymoon window** — fast pins for a few minutes, then it decays as the reprovider re-engages (reproviding 17,875 pins to the DHT via someguy is real work on the 1-CPU cap) and load resumes. So *restart → immediately re-test → "it's fixed!"* would be a false positive. And it won't touch a **structural** cause (pinset size, the 1-CPU cap, or API/SDK code).

**To use it as a real signal, the sequence matters:**
1. Restart Kubo
2. Let it idle ~10–15 min (reprovider scheduled, caches warm, post-GC compaction advances)
3. One clean `upload-throughput@50`

If perf returns to ~baseline **and holds** → the cause was transient runtime state, and periodic restart (or reprovider tuning) is a legit mitigation. If it spikes good then **decays** → structural (pinset/CPU/code), and restart only buys minutes.

On GC: it's already run today (#547 tracks automating it), and the recheck showed GC alone didn't restore throughput — so treat GC as hygiene, not the perf fix.

Want me to run that restart-then-settle-then-retest sequence? It's a ~30s staging blip plus a ~15 min idle — your call on timing since you're mid-deploy. I won't touch it until you say go.

> DEVELOPER

could someguy be the cause of the slowdown?

> AGENT

Good question — and it's worth checking properly rather than reasoning alone. someguy is the delegated-routing sidecar (accelerated DHT), co-located with Kubo on the 2-vCPU box (each capped at 1 CPU). The crux is: **is it on the *synchronous* upload/pin path, or only the IPNS publish/resolve path — and is it healthy or falling back?**

Per ultracode I'll fan out a code-path map in parallel while I pull the decisive live evidence from staging (delegated-routing fallback rate, Kubo routing config, someguy health/load).

> AGENT

Strong live evidence is in, and the answer is **no — someguy is not the primary cause** (it can't explain the halved throughput). Here's why:

**Kubo doesn't even use someguy.** `ipfs config Routing` returns `{"DelegatedRouters": ["auto"]}` — Kubo does its own content routing via the public auto routers, *not* the local sidecar. someguy is used only by the **API's IPNS publish/resolve** layer (via `DELEGATED_ROUTING_URL`). So the dominant upload cost — the Kubo **pin** (4.19s, a local blockstore + pinset op) — never touches someguy.

**someguy is mostly healthy, not a bottleneck.** Delegated-routing counters (cumulative over today's load):

| op | primary (someguy) success | fell back to public | fallback rate |
|---|---|---|---|
| publish | 70,161 | 1,839 | **~2.6%** |
| resolve | 4,683 | 187 | **~3.8%** |

97%+ of publishes and 96%+ of resolves succeed on the local someguy primary; only a small fraction hit the slow public `delegated-ipfs.dev`. someguy is idle now (CPU 1.4%, mem 667 MiB/2 GiB — comfortable). Its logs are *noisy* (25k lines/3h: libp2p identify failures, "cannot reserve inbound connection: resource limit exceeded", DNS misses) but that's normal accelerated-DHT churn, not failure.

**Where someguy *does* cost you (secondary):** it's on the IPNS publish path (publish mean 1.42s, up from 0.83s), and the ~2.6–3.8% fallbacks add public-internet round-trips to those ops. But in the SDK's upload pipeline the IPNS publishes run *concurrently* with — and are mostly masked behind — the two ~4.4s pins (ciphertext + folder metadata). Even if someguy publish were instant, upload latency would drop maybe ~15–20%, not 2×. So it's worth tuning for IPNS *tail* latency, but it's not the regression.

**The regression is still pin-bound:** Kubo pin 1.37s → 4.19s, a local op gated by the 1-CPU cap + the grown pinset/datastore state — independent of someguy.

I have the someguy-role code-map workflow running in parallel to confirm the one thing I'm asserting from config+architecture rather than a direct code read: that the server-side `pin` operation isn't synchronously blocking on any routing/provide call. I'll fold that in when it lands — but the live evidence already rules someguy out as the main driver.

> AGENT

Workflow confirms it — and tightens the conclusion further (with a correction to my own preliminary estimate).

## someguy is definitively ruled out as the regression cause

The code-path map confirms:

1. **Not on the pin path.** The `pin` timer wraps exactly one call — `ipfsProvider.pinFile()` → `POST {kubo}/api/v0/add?pin=true` against the local Kubo node (`local.provider.ts:49`). No delegated-routing call, no synchronous DHT-provide. Kubo has no `Routing.Type` override, so announcing the CID happens in Kubo's **background** reprovider after `add` returns. The 4.19s pin latency is pure local Kubo add+pin.

2. **Correcting my earlier estimate:** I said someguy might cost ~15–20% via IPNS publish. That's wrong — **IPNS publish to someguy is fire-and-forget** (`ipns.service.ts`: DB upsert first, then `delegatedRouting.publish()` as a *detached* promise; the response never waits for DHT propagation). So someguy contributes **~0** to upload latency, not 15–20%.

3. The **only** place someguy is synchronously awaited is `GET /ipns/resolve` — a read path, and even there it's DB-cache-backed (falls through to the Postgres cache on `BAD_GATEWAY`). Not the upload bottleneck.

So the 2.6%/3.8% fallback rates and the noisy someguy logs are a **separate, background IPNS-propagation concern** (worth tracking for discoverability), but they have nothing to do with the halved upload throughput.

## Where that leaves the diagnosis

The regression is now isolated with high confidence to **Kubo's local pin operation: 1.37s → 4.19s**, gated by the 1-CPU cap and the grown pinset/pebbleds state. Everything else (someguy, datastore block-garbage, compose config, box CPU) is ruled out.

**The one decisive test left** to split the two remaining sub-causes — pure 1-CPU concurrency-queuing vs. higher per-pin cost from the 17,875-pin pinset:

- Let staging idle ~15–30 min (reprovide/compaction settle), then **`time ipfs add` a single small file at idle** (no concurrency).
  - ~tens of ms → the 4s is **concurrency queuing on 1 CPU** → fix is raising Kubo's CPU cap.
  - ~1–2s even at idle → **per-pin cost is structurally high** (pinset size / pebbleds) → fix is reducing real pin count and/or datastore tuning.
- Plus one clean `upload-throughput@50` after idle for a steady-state throughput number.

Want me to run that idle single-pin test (+ optional Kubo restart first, per your earlier question) once staging has settled? That's the last step to turn "it's the pin path" into a specific, actionable fix.

Done — Kubo is restarted with the higher memory cap and now settling.

**State:** ipfs cap **3G** (was 2G), CPU still 1 core, fresh daemon (0 restarts), healthy. VPS compose backed up; change is temporary (reverts on next deploy).

**Settle timer running (~25 min).** When it fires I'll run, against the warmed steady-state Kubo:

1. **Single-pin-at-idle** — `time ipfs add`+pin a ~250 KB file, a few sequential reps, zero concurrency. This is the clean isolator:
   - **~tens of ms** → the 4 s pin latency was pure **1-CPU concurrency-queuing** → next lever is the CPU cap, and the memory bump likely won't have moved throughput much.
   - **~1–2 s even at idle** → **per-pin cost is structurally high** (pinset/pebbleds) → memory/datastore tuning is the right lever.
2. **`upload-throughput@50`** — steady-state throughput vs the two anchors: 19.2 baseline (15.10/s, p50 3,242 ms) and today's post-GC (8.85/s, p50 5,537 ms). Tells us whether the 3G bump + restart actually recovered throughput.

I'll report both with the interpretation when the window closes — no need to prompt me.

```
===== apps/api/src/vault/entities/pinned-cid.entity.ts =====
import {
  Entity,
  PrimaryGeneratedColumn,
  Column,
  CreateDateColumn,
  ManyToOne,
  JoinColumn,
  Index,
  Unique,
} from 'typeorm';
import { User } from '../../auth/entities/user.entity';

@Entity('pinned_cids')
@Unique(['userId', 'cid'])
export class PinnedCid {
  @PrimaryGeneratedColumn('uuid')
  id!: string;

  @Index()
  @Column({ type: 'uuid', name: 'user_id' })
  userId!: string;

  @ManyToOne(() => User, { onDelete: 'CASCADE' })
  @JoinColumn({ name: 'user_id' })
  user!: User;

  /**
   * IPFS CID (Content Identifier) for the pinned content
   * CIDv1 format (base32 encoded)
   */
  @Column({ type: 'varchar', length: 255 })
  cid!: string;

  /**
   * Size of the pinned content in bytes
   * Used for quota tracking (500 MiB limit)
   */
  @Column({ type: 'bigint', name: 'size_bytes' })
  sizeBytes!: string; // TypeORM returns bigint as string

  @CreateDateColumn({ name: 'pinned_at' })
  pinnedAt!: Date;
}
```

> AGENT

Done — and the result is cleaner (and more concerning) than the BYO theory predicted. **All 31 are non-BYO**, so none are the benign "pinned on the user's own node" case.

## When they were added

All between **2026-03-28 and 2026-05-26** — none recent (latest is ~4 weeks ago):

| date | count |
|---|---|
| 2026-03-28 | 10 |
| 2026-03-29 | 1 |
| 2026-03-30 | 1 |
| 2026-04-01 | 1 |
| 2026-04-02 | 10 |
| 2026-04-03 | 4 |
| 2026-04-06 | 1 |
| 2026-04-14 | 1 |
| 2026-05-26 | 2 |

So this is **not** from today's deploy or load tests — it's accumulated historical drift. The two clusters (Mar 28, Apr 2) look like specific testing/dev sessions.

## What they are

- **All non-BYO** (`is_byo_user = f`) → they genuinely *should* be pinned on CipherBox's Kubo.
- **All tiny**: 16 B – 36 KB, **0.1 MB total**. All `bafkrei…` = CIDv1 **raw** single-block objects (small encrypted content/metadata blobs).
- **Genuinely gone, not just unpinned**: offline `block stat` on a 15-CID sample → *every one* "block was not found locally." Kubo has neither the pin nor the block.

## Likely mechanism (and an honest note about my GC)

These are **stale `pinned_cids` rows**: at some point the Kubo pin was lost (a failed/partial unpin that removed the pin but left the DB row, or a repo hiccup) **without** deleting the DB row. The blocks then lingered as *unpinned garbage* until a GC swept them. They were already unpinned before today — so my `ipfs repo gc` didn't *cause* the drift, but for any of these whose garbage block was still present this morning, **the GC cleared the last local copy.** The root cause is a DB↔Kubo reconciliation gap that predates today (the timestamps prove it).

## Why this matters for #547

This is the inverse of the orphan drift, and it has a sharper edge: **the system has no auto-repair for the "missing" direction** (the drift report is read-only). If these map to *real* user files, those files are now unfetchable (private encrypted blocks, almost certainly only ever stored on this Kubo). And it's a direct caution for the periodic-GC plan — **aggressive auto-GC must be paired with a reconcile step** (re-pin or prune stale rows), or GC will keep finalizing exactly this kind of loss.

The severity hinges on one thing I haven't checked yet: **are these 31 owned by real users or leftover test accounts?** (Test accounts CASCADE-delete their `pinned_cids` on deletion, so these surviving rows suggest real users — worth confirming.) Want me to pull the owner emails + file references for these 31 and check whether the content is still re-fetchable from the public DHT? That tells us if it's real data loss or just stale bookkeeping. (Settle timer's still running, so now's a good time.)

> AGENT

Decisive result — **the single pin is fast at idle (~0.18s, essentially identical to the 0.16s exec overhead → pure pin work is tens of ms).** So the 4.19s pin latency under load is **pure 1-CPU concurrency-queuing**, not per-pin cost. Per-pin work and pinset size are *not* the bottleneck — the **1.0 CPU cap** is. (Also telling: idle Kubo mem is now just 243 MiB / 3 GiB — so **memory was never the constraint**; the bump likely won't move throughput. CPU is the lever.)

```json
{
  "command": "cd /Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box\nBODY=$(cat <<'EOF'\n## ⚠️ Prerequisite: reconcile DB↔Kubo pin drift *before* enabling aggressive periodic GC\n\nDuring the 2026-06-22 investigation we found **31 `pinned_cids` rows whose content Kubo no longer has pinned** — and the blocks are gone locally too (confirmed via offline `block stat`). These are stale DB rows where the Kubo recursive pin was lost without the row being cleaned up (pinned_at spans 2026-03-28 → 2026-05-26; ~0.1 MB total; all 3 owners are dev/owner test accounts, so no third-party loss in this instance).\n\n**Why this blocks naive auto-GC:** aggressive periodic GC deletes any *unpinned* block. If a `pinned_cids` row has lost its Kubo recursive pin (drift), GC deletes the last local copy and turns recoverable drift into permanent loss. The existing `drift-report` job is read-only and only counts the **orphan** direction (Kubo-pinned but not in DB) — there is **no detection or auto-repair for the \"missing\" direction** (in DB but not pinned on Kubo).\n\n**Required before/with enabling auto-GC:**\n\n1. A reconciliation pass that, for every `pinned_cids` row, verifies a matching Kubo recursive pin and either (a) re-pins the content if still retrievable, or (b) prunes the stale row and refunds quota if the content is genuinely gone.\n2. Run reconciliation **before** the first aggressive GC, then on a schedule alongside GC.\n3. Extend `drift-report` to also detect/report (and optionally repair) the \"missing\" direction, not just orphans.\n\nWithout step 1, turning on `--enable-gc` / a GC cron would keep finalizing exactly this kind of loss.\nEOF\n)\nfor i in 1 2 3 4; do env -u GITHUB_TOKEN gh issue comment 547 -R FSM1/cipher-box -b \"$BODY\" 2>&1 && break; sleep 4; done",

}
```

> AGENT

All three threads handled:

**Owners — answered.** The 31 missing blobs belong to 3 existing dev/owner test accounts (`dev…@cipherbox.local` ×10, your own `mya…@gmail.com` ×20, a wallet ×1), 0.1 MB total, Mar 28–May 26. No third-party data loss — but it confirms the DB↔Kubo "missing" drift bug (stale `pinned_cids` rows after Kubo lost the pin, no auto-repair).

**#547 — reconciliation note posted** ([comment](https://github.com/FSM1/cipher-box/issues/547#issuecomment-4774053720)): aggressive auto-GC must be preceded/paired by a reconcile pass (re-pin or prune stale rows), since GC otherwise finalizes exactly this kind of loss; and `drift-report` should be extended to detect the "missing" direction, not just orphans.

**Root cause sharpened — it's the CPU cap, not memory or datastore.** The single-pin-at-idle test settles it:

| | value |
|---|---|
| exec/CLI overhead baseline | ~0.16 s |
| add+pin at idle (256 KB) | ~0.18 s → **pure pin work ≈ tens of ms** |
| idle Kubo mem (3 G cap) | **243 MiB** |

A pin is trivially fast with no contention. The 4.19 s under load is **pure concurrency-queuing through Kubo's 1.0-CPU cap**. Memory was never the constraint (so the 3 G bump likely won't move throughput), and pinset/datastore size isn't it either. The lever is **CPU** — Kubo gets 1 of the box's 2 cores.

**Final test running** — clean post-restart `upload-throughput@50` on the fresh, settled, 3 G box ([run 27990891321](https://github.com/FSM1/cipher-box/actions/runs/27990891321)). It answers the last open question:
- recovers toward **~15 ops/s** → the earlier 8.85/s was confounded by post-GC compaction + 2 h of load-fatigue; steady-state is ~fine.
- still **~8–9 ops/s** → a real steady-state regression; since idle pins are fast, it's CPU contention worse than March, and the fix is more cores (raise the cap / bigger box), not memory.

I'll deliver the verdict + a consolidated wrap-up (with the durable fixes) when it lands — no need to prompt me.

> DEVELOPER

Would the CPU contention be caused by kubo keeping track of a lot more objects?

> AGENT

Sharp question — and the answer is nuanced: **not via per-pin cost, but plausibly via background work that scales with object count.** My idle test already rules out the first mechanism (a single pin is ~tens of ms even with 17,875 pins, so the pinset doesn't make each pin slower). But Kubo also runs **background maintenance that scales with object count** — chiefly the **reprovider** (re-announcing every pinned CID to the DHT) and the **provider queue**. On a 1-core cap, CPU spent reproviding a large, growing pinset is CPU *not* available to serve concurrent incoming pins → worse queuing under load. That would explain why the same 1-CPU cap yields worse under-load latency now than in March.

> AGENT

That's the answer — **your hypothesis is confirmed, with a specific mechanism.** `ipfs stats reprovide` is the smoking gun:

- **CIDs scheduled for providing: 76,300** (the ~17.9k pin roots + their DAG children) — vs "a couple hundred" at the 19.2 baseline. ~100–400× growth.
- Provider worker pool **near-saturated**: 14/16 active, burst workers maxed (1 queued), 2.6k in the provide queue.
- That background DHT-announcing consumes **~30–70% of Kubo's single core *at idle*** (CPU samples 54% / 73% / 27%, with no load test running).

So it's exactly as you suspected — **not** per-pin cost (idle pin is still ~tens of ms), but the **provider/reprovider system scaling with object count**. On a 1.0-CPU cap, half the core is now eaten by announcing 76k CIDs to the DHT, leaving far less to serve concurrent pin requests → queuing → multi-second pins under load.

## Final test closes the loop

post-restart `upload@50` (fresh daemon, 3G cap, settled): **9.1 ops/s, p50 5,571 ms, 0 errors.** The full ladder:

| run | throughput @50 | p50 |
|---|---|---|
| 19.2 baseline (Mar) | **15.10/s** | 3,242 ms |
| today, pre-GC | 7.71/s | 6,364 ms |
| post-GC | 8.85/s | 5,537 ms |
| **post-restart + 3G mem** | **9.1/s** | 5,571 ms |

GC didn't help, memory bump didn't help, restart didn't help — **all three ruled out empirically.** The regression is persistent (~40% below baseline) and CPU-starvation-driven. (And the 9.1/s is if anything slightly *pessimistic* — it ran while the restart's provide-backlog catch-up was re-announcing all 76k CIDs, eating ~half the core.)

This also finally explains **19.2 (15/s) vs now (9/s) on the identical 1-CPU cap**: in March the provide set was ~hundreds (negligible background CPU → near-full core served pins); now 76k CIDs consume ~half the core. Same cap, less of it available.

## Bottom line for your original question

The recent API/SDK changes did **not** regress the upload path — server-side pin is the cost, and it's Kubo-local. The throughput drop is an **infrastructure scaling issue**: Kubo's provider on a capped core as the pinned set grew ~100×.

## Fix levers (now that memory/GC/restart are out)

1. **Tune the reprovider** (highest leverage, object-count-aware): config is default (`{}`) = strategy "all" (announces *every* block). Switch to `Reprovider.Strategy: "roots"` (announce only pin roots → ~76k down to ~18k) and/or lengthen `Reprovider.Interval`. Or **delegate providing to someguy** (it's the accelerated-DHT sidecar that exists for exactly this) so Kubo's core stops doing DHT announces.
2. **More CPU for Kubo** — raise the 1.0 cap and/or a bigger box. But providing keeps growing with objects, so do #1 regardless.
3. **Shrink the pinned set** — much of the 17.9k is dev/test churn (staging); a cleanup cuts the providing workload.
4. **Revert the 3G mem bump** — it didn't help (idle mem 243 MiB); no reason to make it permanent. It auto-reverts on next deploy anyway.

Want me to **capture this as a dedicated perf issue** (Kubo reprovider scaling → upload throughput regression, with these numbers and the reprovider-tuning fix), and/or **revert the 3G cap** on staging now?

> DEVELOPER

I think that utilizing both the `Reprovider.Strategy: "roots"` and delegate provision to someguy should be documented - kubo is load bearing so I think that actually it deserves the extra memory.

> AGENT

Makes sense — Kubo is load-bearing, so the headroom is justified (it hit 83% of the 2G cap under load; 3G is sensible insurance even though it's not the throughput fix). I'll make the memory permanent in the repo and document both provider fixes. Let me read the right doc home (CAPACITY.md) and check the base compose limits first.
