116	---
117	
118	## 2. Infrastructure Bottlenecks
119	
120	### 2.1 Kubo IPFS Node
121	
122	Kubo's `pin add` operation is the **dominant bottleneck**, consuming ~95% of upload endpoint latency. Each `uploadFile` SDK call requires 3 sequential pin operations (ciphertext, file metadata, folder metadata), totaling ~4-5s server-side at mean.
123	
124	**Optimization impact (pebbleds datastore):**
125	
126	| Metric               | flatfs (pre) | pebbleds (post) | Change     |
127	| -------------------- | ------------ | --------------- | ---------- |
128	| Pin mean latency     | 1.73s        | 1.37s           | -20.8%     |
129	| Pin p50 (estimated)  | ~1.7s        | ~1.0s           | -41%       |
130	| IPNS publish mean    | ~120ms       | ~50ms           | -58%       |
131	| 75-client errors     | 10           | 0               | Eliminated |
132	| 75-client throughput | 2.44 ops/s   | 3.51 ops/s      | +43.9%     |
133	
134	Concurrent pins from multiple clients cause contention on the Kubo datastore. At 50 clients, throughput plateaus at ~3.4-3.7 ops/s locally and ~15-19 ops/s on staging. At 75 clients with flatfs, errors begin appearing; pebbleds eliminates this failure mode entirely.
135	
136	**Memory and CPU:** Kubo uses Go's goroutine scheduler. Pin operations are I/O-bound on the datastore. The pebbleds LSM-tree datastore batches writes, reducing I/O contention under concurrent load.
137	
138	### 2.2 PostgreSQL
139	
140	Auth lookups, IPNS record cache reads/writes, and vault operations are fast:
141	
142	| Operation        | Mean Latency |
143	| ---------------- | ------------ |
144	| Vault lookup     | < 5ms        |
145	| Quota check      | < 5ms        |
146	| IPNS DB resolve  | 23-36ms      |
147	| IPNS DB upsert   | ~50ms        |
148	| Auth login (JWT) | 104-155ms    |
149	
150	PostgreSQL is **not the bottleneck** at current scale. The `folder_ipns` table grows linearly with `users x folders`, but even at 200 concurrent clients, DB operations remain sub-100ms.
151	
152	**Connection pooling:** TypeORM manages the connection pool. Default pool size handles current concurrency levels without exhaustion.
153	
154	### 2.3 API Server (NestJS)
155	
156	Request processing overhead is minimal (~5-10ms per request excluding downstream IPFS/IPNS calls). The API server acts primarily as a pass-through to Kubo for IPFS operations and to PostgreSQL for IPNS record management.
157	
158	- **Rate limiting:** ThrottlerGuard protects against abuse. Load tests bypass throttling via `THROTTLE_BYPASS_SECRET` in test mode.
159	- **Connection handling:** NestJS handles concurrent HTTP connections efficiently. No observed issues at 200 concurrent clients.
160	- **CPU:** Not a bottleneck. Server-side processing time per request is dominated by Kubo pin latency, not application logic.
161	
162	### 2.4 Redis
163	
164	Used for rate limiting state and session management. Sub-millisecond operations. Not a bottleneck at any observed concurrency level.
165	
166	### 2.5 Someguy (Delegated Routing Sidecar)
167	
168	IPNS publish DHT propagation happens asynchronously and does not block client responses. DHT propagation latency:
169	
170	| Outcome | Mean  | Count   | Error Rate |
171	| ------- | ----- | ------- | ---------- |
172	| Success | 838ms | 147,285 | --         |
173	| Error   | 17.2s | 324     | 0.22%      |
174	
175	DHT propagation errors do not affect client-facing operations (fire-and-forget). Someguy's warm DHT is critical for IPNS resolution performance in non-API contexts (recovery tool, TEE republishing).
176	
177	---
178	
179	## 3. Scaling Recommendations
180	
181	### 3.1 When to Scale
182	
183	| Signal                                     | Threshold              | Action                                     |
184	| ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
185	| Upload p95 > 5s consistently (staging)     | Kubo pin contention    | Add second Kubo node or upgrade hardware   |
186	| Upload p95 > 15s consistently (staging)    | Kubo saturated         | Kubo cluster mode or dedicated pin workers |
187	| API response time p95 > 500ms (non-IPFS)   | API CPU bound          | Add API replica behind load balancer       |
188	| PostgreSQL connections > 80% pool          | DB saturated           | Increase pool size or add read replica     |
189	| Kubo datastore > 80% disk                  | Storage full           | Expand volume or enable garbage collection |
190	| IPNS publish queue depth > 100             | Publish backlog        | Tune Kubo worker concurrency               |
191	| Mixed workload error rate > 1%             | Infrastructure ceiling | Scale horizontally (Kubo + API)            |
192	| Throughput plateaus despite adding clients | Single-node ceiling    | Horizontal scaling required                |
193	
194	### 3.2 How to Scale
195	
196	**Kubo IPFS Node:**
197	
198	- **Vertical:** Increase CPU/RAM/SSD. Pebbleds datastore benefits significantly from faster I/O. Move from HDD to NVMe SSD for pin operations.
199	- **Horizontal:** Run multiple Kubo nodes behind the API. Each API instance pins to a designated Kubo node. Requires content routing coordination (IPFS Cluster or custom routing).
200	- **Cluster mode:** [IPFS Cluster](https://cluster.ipfs.io/) adds pinning coordination but introduces operational complexity. Recommended only when single-node Kubo reaches sustained saturation.
201	
202	**API Server (NestJS):**
203	
204	- The API is stateless (session state in Redis, no in-memory state). Adding replicas behind Caddy or nginx is straightforward.
205	- Ensure all replicas share the same Redis instance for rate limiting and session consistency.
206	- Database connection pool per replica needs coordination (total connections = replicas x pool_size).
207	
208	**PostgreSQL:**
209	
210	- **Read replicas:** IPNS cache reads can be directed to read replicas. Write scaling is unnecessary at current volume.
211	- **Connection pooling:** Use PgBouncer if connection count exceeds PostgreSQL's `max_connections`.
212	- **Sharding:** Premature for a tech demo. Only consider if user count exceeds ~100,000.
213	
214	**TEE Republishing:**
215	
216	- Independent scaling path. Add TEE workers to increase IPNS republish batch throughput.
217	- Each TEE worker processes a batch of IPNS names every 6 hours.
218	- Batch duration grows linearly with IPNS name count. Monitor `cipherbox_republish_batch_duration_seconds`.
219	
220	---
221	
222	## 4. Growth Projections
223	
224	### 4.1 Storage Growth
225	
226	All files are encrypted with unique keys, so IPFS deduplication does not apply.
227	
228	**Formula:**
229	
230	```text
231	storage_bytes = users * avg_files_per_user * avg_encrypted_file_size * 1.05
232	```
233	
234	The 1.05 multiplier accounts for ~5% metadata overhead (file metadata CIDs, folder metadata CIDs, IPNS records).
235	
236	| Users  | Files/User | Avg File Size | IPFS Storage | Monthly Growth (10 files/user/mo) |
237	| ------ | ---------- | ------------- | ------------ | --------------------------------- |
238	| 100    | 100        | 500KB         | ~5 GB        | ~500 MB/mo                        |
239	| 1,000  | 100        | 500KB         | ~50 GB       | ~5 GB/mo                          |
240	| 10,000 | 100        | 500KB         | ~500 GB      | ~50 GB/mo                         |
241	| 1,000  | 100        | 5MB           | ~500 GB      | ~50 GB/mo                         |
242	
243	### 4.2 IPNS Name Growth
244	
245	Each user has:
246	
247	- 1 vault blob IPNS name
248	- N folder IPNS names (1 per folder including root)
249	- M file IPNS names (1 per file)
250	
251	**Formula:**
252	
253	```text
254	ipns_names = users * (1 + avg_folders_per_user + avg_files_per_user)
255	```
256	
257	| Users  | Folders/User | Files/User | Total IPNS Names | TEE Republish Batch Size |
258	| ------ | ------------ | ---------- | ---------------- | ------------------------ |
259	| 100    | 10           | 100        | ~11,100          | ~11,100 every 6h         |
260	| 1,000  | 10           | 100        | ~111,000         | ~111,000 every 6h        |
261	| 10,000 | 10           | 100        | ~1,110,000       | ~1,110,000 every 6h      |
262	
263	TEE republish batch duration scales linearly with IPNS name count. At current Someguy publish throughput (~15 ops/s), 111,000 names would take ~2 hours to republish -- within the 6-hour window. At 1.1M names, multiple TEE workers or batching optimizations would be needed.
264	
265	### 4.3 Database Growth
266	
267	| Table         | Growth Driver             | Rows at 1,000 Users | Rows at 10,000 Users |
268	| ------------- | ------------------------- | ------------------- | -------------------- |
269	| users         | 1 per user                | 1,000               | 10,000               |
270	| vaults        | 1 per user                | 1,000               | 10,000               |
271	| folder_ipns   | users x (folders + files) | ~111,000            | ~1,110,000           |
272	| device_tokens | users x avg_devices       | ~2,000              | ~20,000              |
273	
274	The `folder_ipns` table is the largest growth driver. With proper indexing (already in place on `user_id`, with a unique constraint on `(user_id, ipns_name)`), queries remain efficient at millions of rows.
275	
276	### 4.4 Cost Estimates (Single Server Deployment)
277	
278	These are rough estimates for a self-hosted tech demo deployment, not production SLAs.
279	
280	| Component     | 100 Users      | 1,000 Users     | 10,000 Users     |
281	| ------------- | -------------- | --------------- | ---------------- |
282	| VPS (CPU/RAM) | $10-20/mo      | $40-80/mo       | $150-300/mo      |
283	| Storage (SSD) | $5/mo (50GB)   | $25/mo (250GB)  | $100/mo (1TB)    |
284	| Bandwidth     | $5/mo          | $20/mo          | $100/mo          |
285	| Domain/SSL    | $15/yr         | $15/yr          | $15/yr           |
286	| **Total**     | **~$20-30/mo** | **~$85-125/mo** | **~$350-500/mo** |
287	
288	**Notes:**
289	
290	- VPS pricing based on Hostinger/Hetzner tier estimates
291	- Storage assumes SSD volumes at ~$0.10/GB/mo
292	- Bandwidth depends heavily on download patterns and provider egress pricing
293	- Does not include TEE infrastructure costs (Phala Cloud pricing varies)
294	
295	---
296	
297	## 5. Load Test Thresholds
298	
299	Automated pass/fail thresholds are defined in `tests/load/src/harness/thresholds.ts` and integrated into all 8 load test scenarios. Thresholds are 2-3x observed baselines to avoid CI flakiness while still catching significant regressions.
300	
301	| Scenario           | Operation    | p95 Threshold | Error Rate Max | Rationale                                 |
302	| ------------------ | ------------ | ------------- | -------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
303	| upload-throughput  | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 5%             | 19.2 p95 was 2,841ms at 5 clients (~3.5x) |
304	| mixed-workload     | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 10%            | Mixed has higher error rate historically  |
305	| mixed-workload     | createFolder | 5,000ms       | 10%            | 19.2 p95 was 936ms at 5 clients (~5x)     |
306	| ipns-publish-storm | createFolder | 10,000ms      | 10%            | IPNS contention scenario, high variance   |
307	| sustained-load     | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 5%             | Same as upload-throughput                 |
308	| sustained-load     | createFolder | 5,000ms       | 5%             | Folder ops should stay fast               |
309	| spike-test (burst) | uploadFile   | 15,000ms      | 15%            | Spike: intentional overload               |
310	| spike-test (burst) | createFolder | 15,000ms      | 15%            | Spike: intentional overload               |
311	
312	**How thresholds work:**
313	
314	1. Each scenario runs its workload and collects `OperationMetrics` via `aggregateAndReport()`
315	2. `checkThresholds()` compares observed p95 latency and error rate against the threshold table
316	3. If any threshold is breached, the test fails with a descriptive violation message listing the operation, observed value, and threshold
317	4. The CI workflow (`load-test.yml`) runs via `workflow_dispatch` (manual trigger), not on every push
318	
319	**Updating thresholds:** When baselines shift significantly (new infrastructure, new optimizations), update the threshold values in each scenario file. Keep thresholds at 2-3x observed values to maintain a buffer against normal variance.
320	
321	---
322	
323	## 7. Retention Consequence of BYO Advisory Rows
324	
325	### 7.1 Background
326	
327	CipherBox supports two storage modes for each file CID in `pinned_cids`:
328	
329	- **Hosted rows**: CIDs pinned via the CipherBox relay (`isByoUser = false`). The CipherBox
330	  Kubo node physically holds the content.
331	- **BYO advisory rows**: CIDs registered by BYO-IPFS users (`isByoUser = true`). These rows
332	  track what the user claims is pinned on their own infrastructure; the CipherBox Kubo node
333	  does NOT hold these CIDs.
334	
335	### 7.2 The Retention Consequence
336	
337	The `guardedUnpin` refcount query counts ALL `pinned_cids` rows for a given CID regardless of
338	origin — both hosted and BYO advisory rows contribute to the refcount (D-07 design, WR-07
339	disposition: accepted).
340	
341	**Consequence:** If a BYO advisory row exists for a CID that is also held by the CipherBox Kubo
342	node (hosted), deleting the hosted row will NOT trigger a physical unpin of the CID from Kubo
343	until the BYO advisory row is also removed. The CID is retained in Kubo for as long as any BYO
344	advisory row references it.
345	
346	**When this occurs:**
347	
348	1. User A uploads a file via the CipherBox relay (hosted `pinned_cids` row created).
349	2. User B (BYO-IPFS mode) registers the same CID (advisory `pinned_cids` row created).
350	3. User A deletes their file — their hosted row is removed and their quota is decremented
351	   immediately (D-03), but the CID remains pinned in Kubo because User B's advisory row
352	   keeps the refcount above zero.
353	4. Kubo storage is freed only when User B also removes their advisory row.
354	
355	**Impact on capacity planning:** In environments with significant BYO-IPFS usage, Kubo storage
356	consumption may exceed what is attributable to hosted users alone. Monitor
357	`cipherbox_pending_unpin_queue_depth` and `cipherbox_drift_orphaned_pins_total` for divergence
358	between DB-accounted and Kubo-actual pin counts.
359	
360	**Operator action:** No action is required — this is intentional design. If an operator needs
361	to reclaim Kubo storage held by a BYO-referenced CID, they must first remove the BYO advisory
362	row (e.g., by having the BYO user delete their reference), which will allow the next
363	`drain-pending-unpins` BullMQ run to physically unpin it.
364

```
    environment:
      - IPFS_PATH=/data/ipfs
      # pebbleds: LSM-tree datastore for faster concurrent write throughput (Phase 19.2)
```

> AGENT

The investigation nails it. The test harness creates accounts as `${prefix}-${label}-${ts}@example.com` (test-harness.ts:71), and staging has **365 `@example.com` accounts holding 16,016 pins — ~90% of the entire pinned set** (i.e., ~90% of the provide workload starving Kubo). These are leftover test accounts from interrupted/failed load-test runs that never got cleaned up. That's the high-impact #3 target.

Deletion goes through `DELETE /auth/account` → `authService.deleteAccount(userId)`. Let me read that to confirm it unpins from Kubo properly (raw SQL would orphan the pins):

```
225	  async deleteAccount(userId: string): Promise<{ success: boolean }> {
226	    // Fetch all pinned CIDs before cascade deletes the records
227	    const pinnedCids = await this.pinnedCidRepository.find({
228	      where: { userId },
229	      select: ['cid'],
230	    });
231	
232	    // Unpin all content from Kubo (best-effort, don't block deletion on failure)
233	    if (pinnedCids.length > 0) {
234	      const results = await Promise.allSettled(
235	        pinnedCids.map((pin) => this.ipfsProvider.unpinFile(pin.cid))
236	      );
237	      const failed = results.filter((r) => r.status === 'rejected').length;
238	      this.logger.log(
239	        `Unpinned ${pinnedCids.length - failed}/${pinnedCids.length} CIDs for user ${userId}` +
240	          (failed > 0 ? ` (${failed} failed)` : '')
241	      );
242	    }
243	
244	    // Delete user row — cascade handles all related DB records
245	    const result = await this.userRepository.delete(userId);
246	    if (result.affected === 0) {
247	      throw new BadRequestException('Account not found');
248	    }
249	    this.logger.log(`Account deleted: userId=${userId}`);
250	    return { success: true };
251	  }
252	
253	  /**
254	   * Refresh tokens by searching for the matching refresh token across all users.
255	   * This allows refresh without requiring the (possibly expired) access token.
256	   */
257	  async refreshByToken(refreshToken: string): Promise<RefreshServiceResult> {
258	    // Find candidate tokens by prefix for O(1) lookup instead of O(N) Argon2 scan
259	    const prefix = refreshToken.substring(0, 16);
260	    const tokens = await this.refreshTokenRepository.find({
261	      where: {
262	        tokenPrefix: prefix,
263	        revokedAt: IsNull(),
264	      },
265	      relations: ['user'],
266	    });
267	
268	    // Find matching token by verifying against hashes
269	    let validToken: RefreshToken | null = null;
270	    for (const token of tokens) {
271	      // Skip expired tokens
272	      if (token.expiresAt < new Date()) {
273	        continue;
274	      }
275	      try {
276	        if (await argon2.verify(token.tokenHash, refreshToken)) {
277	          validToken = token;
278	          break;
279	        }
280	      } catch {
281	        // argon2.verify throws on invalid hash format, continue checking
282	        continue;
283	      }
284	    }
285	
286	    if (!validToken) {
287	      throw new UnauthorizedException('Invalid or expired refresh token');
288	    }
289	
290	    // Revoke old token
291	    validToken.revokedAt = new Date();
292	    await this.refreshTokenRepository.save(validToken);
293	
294	    // Create new tokens
295	    const newTokens = await this.tokenService.createTokens(
296	      validToken.userId,
297	      validToken.user.publicKey
298	    );
299	
```

Excellent point — that's the right experimental discipline. The current bloated state (76k provide CIDs, ~50% idle CPU on Kubo) is a perfect, hard-won reproduction. Validating both fixes against it *now* — before cleanup wipes the repro — means we confirm they work without having to re-bloat the system later. Let me hold off on the deletion and instead apply **Fix 1 (`Reprovider.Strategy: roots`)** and probe **Fix 2 (someguy as delegate provider)** feasibility.

Manually migrate 'Reprovider' fields to 'Provide': Reprovider.Strategy -> Provide.Strategy, Reprovider.Interval -> Provide.DHT.Interval. Remove 'Reprovider' from your config. Documentation: https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/blob/master/docs/config.md#provide

> AGENT

Found it — and it's my mistake. Kubo 0.40 **deprecated the `Reprovider` config section** (moved to `Provide`), and it now **FATALs on startup** if `Reprovider.*` is present:

```
FATAL: Deprecated configuration detected. Manually migrate 'Reprovider' fields to 'Provide':
Reprovider.Strategy -> Provide.Strategy ... Remove 'Reprovider' from your config.
```

So setting `Reprovider.Strategy` created the deprecated key → crash loop → staging IPFS down. Fixing now: stop the loop, edit the config to remove `Reprovider` and set the correct **`Provide.Strategy: "roots"`** (which both restores staging *and* applies the intended test), then restart.

> TOOL

> AGENT

Two findings, one of them a correction to the plan:

**Fix 1 (`Provide.Strategy: roots`) — applied and working.** Scheduled CIDs dropped **76,300 → 17,880 (−77%)** immediately. Whether that translates to recovered throughput is what the settle + load test will show.

**Fix 2 (delegate providing to someguy) — not feasible as stated.** `someguy start --help` makes its role clear: it's a **read-side delegated-routing proxy**. Every option is about *proxying lookup requests outward* — `--provider-endpoints` ("proxy provider **requests** to"), `--peer-endpoints`, `--ipns-endpoints`. There's **no capability to accept provider writes/announcements**. someguy answers "who has CID X?" queries by proxying to the DHT; it has nowhere for Kubo to *push* its own content announcements. So Kubo can't offload its providing to someguy — someguy isn't a provide *sink*, only a lookup *source*.

To actually offload announcing you'd need a **provide-accepting delegated router** (an IPNI-style indexer that implements `PUT /routing/v1/providers`), which is a separate service — not someguy. So the realistic levers, in order of effort, are:

1. **`Provide.Strategy: roots`** ✅ (done, −77% already)
2. **Lengthen `Provide.DHT.Interval`** — reprovide less often (trade-off: DHT freshness).
3. **Disable DHT providing entirely** — *if* CipherBox content is only ever retrieved through the API/gateway (not P2P bitswap from arbitrary peers). Worth confirming whether anything (recovery tool, external fetch) actually relies on DHT discovery of CipherBox CIDs — if not, Kubo doesn't need to announce at all, freeing the core completely.
4. **More CPU** (raise the cap / bigger box).

Also — I owned a few-minute staging IPFS outage here (the deprecated `Reprovider.*` key FATALs Kubo 0.40 on boot → crash loop; recovered by migrating to `Provide.Strategy`). The **key correction for the eventual config/docs: it's `Provide.Strategy`, not `Reprovider.Strategy`** in 0.40.

**Settle timer running (~20 min)** for the roots provide catch-up. Then I'll measure idle CPU + `upload-throughput@50` to confirm whether roots alone recovers throughput toward the 15 ops/s baseline (from 9.1), and report. I'll hold the memory/docs PR and the test-account cleanup until we've validated this.

> DEVELOPER

while we wait, can you tell me if cpu can be increased by fractional numbers

> AGENT

Yes — `cpus` is fractional. In Compose `deploy.resources.limits.cpus` (and the older top-level `cpus`) it's a CPU-*time* quota, not a whole-core count. Any positive decimal works:

```yaml
deploy:
  resources:
    limits:
      cpus: '1.5'   # 1.5 cores' worth of CPU time
```

Under the hood it sets CFS `cpu.cfs_quota_us / cpu.cfs_period_us` (e.g. `1.5` → quota 150000 / period 100000). `'0.5'`, `'1.25'`, `'1.75'` are all valid.

Two caveats that matter for our 2-vCPU staging box:

1. **Limits are caps, not reservations — they can oversubscribe.** Right now ipfs `1.0` + someguy `1.0` = the whole box. If I bump ipfs to `1.5`, the sum is `2.5` on a 2-core host. That's allowed: each is just a ceiling. Under simultaneous full load they'd contend for the 2 real cores, but **someguy is essentially idle** (observed ~1.4%), so giving ipfs `1.5`–`2.0` lets it soak up someguy's unused core exactly when it needs it (during a load test). That's effectively free headroom.

2. **You can't exceed physical cores in practice.** Setting ipfs to `'3.0'` on a 2-vCPU box is pointless — it can never get more than 2 cores of real time. So the useful range here is `1.0 → 2.0`.

Given the root cause is Kubo queuing on its 1.0 cap, the cheap experiment is: bump ipfs to `1.5` (or `2.0`, leaving someguy to compete only when it's actually busy) and re-run upload@50. That's an independent lever from `Provide.Strategy: roots` — combining both is likely the real fix. I'll fold a CPU bump into the measurement plan once the settle timer fires.

> DEVELOPER

but wont bumping the cpu and restarting the kubo node mean we need to wait another 15-20 minutes for things to settle again?

> AGENT

No restart needed — that's the key. Changing the CPU cap doesn't require recreating the container, so it doesn't re-trigger the provide sweep or a second settle.

The settle exists only because **restarting** Kubo makes it re-announce its 17,880 pin roots to the DHT on boot (the catch-up provide burst). CPU limits, though, are just a cgroup quota you can change **live on the running container**:

```bash
docker update --cpus 1.5 <ipfs-container>   # applies instantly, no restart
```

This rewrites `cpu.cfs_quota_us` on the already-settled, already-announced container. Kubo doesn't even notice — it just suddenly has more CPU budget. So we stack the CPU lever directly onto the current settled `Provide.Strategy: roots` state and measure both at once, no waiting.

Two notes:
- **It's ephemeral.** `docker update` bypasses compose, so a future `docker compose up`/recreate reverts it to the file's `1.0`. That's fine for the experiment — we persist the real value via the compose-file edit + PR afterward (which *is* a recreate, but that settle happens on a normal deploy anyway, not mid-test).
- We'll grab the exact container name with `docker compose ps` first (likely `cipherbox-staging-ipfs-1`).

So sequence once the timer fires: measure roots-only baseline → `docker update --cpus 1.5` → immediately re-run upload@50. Two data points, one settle.

> DEVELOPER

do you think it makes sense to lower someguy's cpu to 0.5?

> AGENT

Short answer: no — and it's worth being precise about *why*, because the instinct ("free up a core for ipfs") doesn't actually hold under how cgroup limits work.

**Caps are ceilings, not reservations.** someguy's `1.0` cap doesn't *reserve* a core away from ipfs. ipfs already gets all the CPU it wants up to *its own* cap, because the CFS scheduler hands out the physical cores on demand and someguy isn't using its share (~1.4% observed). So lowering someguy `1.0 → 0.5` gives ipfs **nothing** — ipfs is constrained by its own `1.0` cap, not by someguy's allocation. The lever that helps ipfs is raising *ipfs's* cap. That's the whole move.

**The only thing a lower someguy cap does is throttle someguy harder under contention — which is a downside.** someguy is load-bearing for delegated routing (IPNS resolve, find-providers). During the *upload-throughput* test it's near-idle, sure — but mixed/sustained workloads do IPNS and lookup work that routes through it, so it's not reliably idle across all scenarios. Capping it at 0.5 risks starving the routing path during exactly the tests where it's active, which would muddy (or hurt) the numbers we're trying to improve.

**Recommendation:** leave someguy at `1.0`, raise ipfs to `1.5` or `2.0`. Oversubscription (sum 2.5–3.0 on a 2-core box) is fine — limits can sum past physical cores; they only bite during simultaneous peaks, and CFS shares proportionally if that ever happens. someguy's real usage stays low, so ipfs soaks up the idle headroom for free.

If the actual goal were "guarantee ipfs gets priority when both spike," the right tool is **CPU shares/weight** (`cpu_shares`, a relative weight) — give ipfs a heavier weight so it wins contention — *not* a hard cap on someguy. But given someguy's measured idleness, even that's overkill. Just raise ipfs.

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

**Run A (roots @ ipfs=1.0): 8.71 ops/s**, p50 5779ms, p95 8001ms (1000 uploads, 0 errors). The CPU trace is decisive:

```
ipfs CPU during the load window (00:24:23 → 00:26:35):
  55% → 62% → 66% → 105% → 102% → 98% → 102% → 96% → 100% → 101% → 104% ...  (pegged at the 1.0 cap)
someguy during same window: 21% → 49% → 78% → 47% → 35% ... (NOT idle — real routing work)
```

**Two findings:**

1. **Roots alone did *not* recover throughput.** 8.71/s vs the pre-roots 9.1/s — same, within noise. Why: ipfs sits **pegged at its 1.0 cap (100–105%) for the entire load window**. The load-time bottleneck is raw CPU on upload/pin work (bitswap, block writes, hashing) — *not* the background reprovide. Roots cut the background/idle announce cost (good for steady state, 76k→17.9k), but that's not what saturates the core during a test.

2. **someguy is busy during uploads** (spikes to 30–78%) — it's doing provider/routing work, not idling. This confirms it was right *not* to cap it down; ipfs@1.5 + someguy will genuinely contend at peak.

So the CPU cap is the lever that matters. Applying it live and running Run B:

| | Run A (1.0) | Run B (1.5) | Δ |

| throughput | 8.71/s | **10.28/s** | **+18%** |
| p50 | 5779ms | 4894ms | −15% |
| p95 | 8001ms | 6506ms | −19% |
| ipfs CPU peak | ~105% (capped) | 110–147% (uses headroom) | — |

The CPU bump is a **real but modest +18%** — ipfs now climbs into the extra headroom (peaks 110–147%, i.e. up to its 1.5 cap), but it's not the ~50% you'd get from pure CPU scaling. The trace shows why: **someguy contends** — it spikes to 82–85% during load, and on a 2-core box ipfs@1.5 + someguy@0.85 > 2.0, so ipfs gets pulled back to ~80–100% exactly when someguy peaks.

Critically, **10.28/s is still ~32% below the healthy 19.2 baseline (15.10/s)**. So config levers alone don't close the gap — the residual is the **datastore/object bloat itself** (what the test-account cleanup addresses) plus the 2-core ceiling.

One more data point to separate "CPU-bound" from "bloat/IO-bound": **Run C @ ipfs=2.0**. If 2.0 jumps again → a bigger box helps. If 2.0 ≈ 1.5 → someguy contention isn't the wall, we're bloat/IO-bound and **cleanup is the real fix**. Launching:

> DEVELOPER

if cleanup is the fix that has me really worried about scalability - 17k objects is only ~ 7.5k files + folders

> AGENT

Good instinct to push on this — and it makes me want to **correct something I said after Run B**. I called the residual gap "object bloat," but the prior session's own evidence contradicts that, and it changes the scalability story significantly.

**Cleanup is *not* the throughput fix.** The earlier GC already ran the experiment for us: it dropped the datastore **294,811 → 20,038 objects** and throughput **did not move**. Memory bump + restart didn't either. Only the CPU lever moved it. So removing the 365 test accounts will reclaim *disk* and stop garbage accrual — it's hygiene — but it won't buy throughput. I shouldn't have implied otherwise.

**What that means for your 7.5k-files worry:** the node is *not* choking on 7.5k files. Within everything tested (hundreds → 294k objects), throughput is **concurrency-CPU-bound, not object-count-bound**. What you're measuring is a **2-vCPU box absorbing 50 simultaneous SDK uploads** — that's a brutal concurrency ratio that real usage rarely hits against a single node. "10 uploads/s at 50 clients" is a stress figure, not a capacity ceiling on how many files can exist.

**The one path that genuinely scales with file count is provide/DHT CPU** — announcing CIDs to the DHT. That *was* the object-count→slowness link (76k CIDs ate ~50% of the core at idle). `Provide.Strategy: roots` cut it 77% and bounds it to **pin-roots, not total blocks**, so it now grows with files-and-folders rather than every chunk. That's the axis you'd worry about, and it's the one we already defused.

**So what's the 15.1 → 10.3 gap vs the old baseline, if not objects?** Same box, same concurrency, so it's something that *changed*. The leading, measured suspect is **someguy** — it spikes to 82–85% during uploads and contends with ipfs on the 2-core box. If someguy was added after the 19.2 baseline (likely), at baseline ipfs had the box more to itself. That alone could account for most of the gap, and it's a co-location artifact, not a per-object cost. (Kubo v0.40's newer provide system is a secondary suspect.)

**Real scale levers — none of them is "cap files per user":**
- More cores / bigger box (vertical) — the immediate one.
- Separate someguy onto its own box/cores — reclaims the contention we measured.
- Keep `roots` + recurring GC — keeps the object-count-sensitive path flat.
- At large scale: shard pins across multiple Kubo nodes (horizontal), and delegate *providing* to an IPNI-style indexer (someguy can't accept provides, as we found).

To actually nail the residual gap — and directly answer "is this a fixable contention artifact or a real regression?" — the clean next experiment is a **someguy-isolation run**: temporarily cap someguy low (or pause it) and re-run upload@50. If ipfs throughput jumps toward 15/s, the gap is co-location contention (fixable by separating someguy), and your scalability fear is largely unfounded. Run C (ipfs=2.0) is in flight now and partly tests this; I'll propose the someguy-isolation run after it lands.

> AGENT

**Run C (roots @ ipfs=2.0): 9.80 ops/s**, p50 5105ms, p95 7385ms (1 error). The full ladder:

| | A: 1.0 | B: 1.5 | C: 2.0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| throughput | 8.71/s | **10.28/s** | 9.80/s |
| p50 | 5779 | **4894** | 5105 |
| p95 | 8001 | **6506** | 7385 |

**The knee is 1.5 — 2.0 gives nothing back (slightly worse, within noise).** The combined-CPU trace says exactly why: during the load window, **ipfs + someguy together hit 130–176% of the 200% the box has** (samples: sum=156, 162, 176, 170, 165…). When someguy spikes to 62–92%, ipfs gets squeezed to 73–94%. So raising ipfs's *cap* to 2.0 does nothing because the **2 physical cores are the real ceiling**, and they're shared with someguy. We're genuinely out of CPU on this box under 50-client load.

This **confirms the someguy-contention story** from my last message — it's not incidental, it's a co-tenant eating ~0.5–0.9 of the box's 2 cores during uploads. The throughput path forward is **more cores / move someguy off this box**, not higher caps here (proven: C≈B).

Before I propose the isolation run, one thing matters: *why* is someguy so busy during *uploads* (its job is normally read-side routing)? If Kubo is delegating provider announcements to someguy, then someguy's load is intrinsic to the upload path. Let me check the Routing config and set ipfs back to the chosen 1.5:

> AGENT

**Routing finding:** Kubo's `Routing.DelegatedRouters: ["auto"]` — it delegates to the public `delegated-ipfs.dev`, **not** to someguy. someguy runs default (`someguy start`) and is consumed by the **API** (`DELEGATED_ROUTING_URL`), so its upload-time CPU spikes come from the API's per-operation routing calls. It's load-bearing *during* uploads, on the same 2 cores as ipfs.

ipfs is back at the chosen knee (1.5). Now the decisive picture so far:

- **Object count is not the limiter** (GC 294k→20k didn't move throughput).
- **The 2-core box is the wall** — ipfs+someguy saturate it (sum 160–176%); ipfs gets squeezed exactly when someguy spikes.
- **Knee = ipfs 1.5** (+18%); 2.0 buys nothing on this box.

To put a *number* on how much throughput is reclaimable by un-contending ipfs from someguy — i.e. to prove the gap is **sizing, not a per-file wall** — I'm running the isolation test now: ipfs=2.0 + someguy throttled to 0.4 (temporary; restored after). If throughput jumps toward ~15/s, your scalability fear is unfounded and the answer is simply "give ipfs and someguy their own cores."

> AGENT

**Run D (isolation: ipfs=2.0, someguy=0.4): 8.66 ops/s** — *worse* than B (10.28), back at A's level. This is the most informative run of the four, and it's counterintuitive:

- someguy **pegged at its 0.4 cap** the whole window (~41% sustained = throttled).
- The box was **under-saturated** (sum 110–150 vs C's 160–176) — ipfs had spare cores.
- Yet throughput *fell*.

**Interpretation: someguy is not a parasitic co-tenant stealing ipfs's CPU — it's a *required per-upload participant*.** The API calls someguy (delegated routing) on the critical path of each upload. Throttle someguy → the upload pipeline queues behind it → fewer uploads/s → ipfs has *less* to do (hence the lower sum). Freeing CPU for ipfs didn't help because ipfs wasn't the one being starved. **This vindicates your original instinct not to cap someguy.**

Full ladder (all roots, same bloated state, n=1 each):

| Run | ipfs | someguy | ops/s | p50 | note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.0 | 1.0 | 8.71 | 5779 | ipfs pegged at cap |
| **B** | **1.5** | **1.0** | **10.28** | **4894** | **knee — only lever that helped (+18%)** |
| C | 2.0 | 1.0 | 9.80 | 5105 | box saturated, no gain |
| D | 2.0 | 0.4 | 8.66 | 5855 | someguy starved → pipeline bottleneck |

### Verdict on your scalability worry
**It's a sizing problem, not a per-file wall.** The throughput limit is a **2-core box running *two* CPU-hungry services that are *both* exercised per upload** — Kubo (pin/hash/bitswap) + someguy (routing) — under 50-way concurrency. They jointly saturate 2 cores. Object count is irrelevant here (GC 294k→20k proved it); 7.5k files isn't what's slow. The fix is **more cores or splitting the two services onto separate cores/boxes** — standard scaling, and it should recover toward the 15/s baseline.

### Recommended plan
1. **Land the one real win:** `ipfs cpus=1.5`, keep 3G mem, keep `Provide.Strategy: roots` → PR. Best config on this box (+18%, p50/p95 down ~15–19%).
2. **For real headroom:** provision **≥4 vCPU** staging (or move someguy to its own box) — lets ipfs get ~1.5–2 *and* someguy ~1 without contending.
3. **Code lead worth chasing separately:** the API makes a CPU-heavy someguy/delegated-routing call *per upload* on the critical path. Caching/reducing that (e.g. folder-state resolves) could cut per-upload cost **without** more hardware. Likely the biggest lever, and it's a code fix.
4. **Test-account cleanup:** still worth doing — but as **disk hygiene**, not throughput.

Staging is restored to the best-tested live config (ipfs=1.5, someguy=1.0; ephemeral until the PR lands).

Caveat: n=1 per config — the A→B +18% is solid, but I can replicate B/C if you want statistical confidence. Which do you want me to action first — the config PR, the cleanup, or dig into the per-upload someguy call (#3)?

1. `docker/ipfs-init/001-provide-strategy.sh` — Kubo `/container-init.d` script (sourced every boot, idempotent) that sets `Provide.Strategy roots`.
2. Both compose files: mount `./ipfs-init`, `cpus 1.0→1.5`, `memory 2G→3G`.
3. `deploy-staging.yml`: add the script to the scp source list.
4. `docs/CAPACITY.md`: document the findings + fixes (correcting `Reprovider`→`Provide` for 0.40, and that someguy-as-delegate-provider is infeasible).

96	**Key finding:** DHT warm-up matters significantly. Staging someguy (8h uptime) is ~2x faster than local someguy with a cold DHT (10min uptime).
97	
98	### 1.4 Server-Side Pin Latency (Prometheus, Post-Optimization)
99	
100	Kubo pin latency distribution captured from staging after 20,944 pin operations (pebbleds datastore):
101	
102	| Bucket   | Count  | Cumulative % |
103	| -------- | ------ | ------------ |
104	| <= 10ms  | 28     | 0.1%         |
105	| <= 50ms  | 181    | 0.9%         |
106	| <= 100ms | 564    | 2.7%         |
107	| <= 250ms | 2,042  | 9.7%         |
108	| <= 500ms | 4,944  | 23.6%        |
109	| <= 1s    | 9,341  | 44.6%        |
110	| <= 2.5s  | 17,477 | 83.4%        |
111	| <= 5s    | 20,898 | 99.8%        |
112	| <= 10s   | 20,944 | 100%         |
113	
114	**Mean pin latency:** 1.37s (down from 1.73s pre-optimization, -20.8%)
115	
116	---
117	
118	## 2. Infrastructure Bottlenecks
119	
120	### 2.1 Kubo IPFS Node
121	
122	Kubo's `pin add` operation is the **dominant bottleneck**, consuming ~95% of upload endpoint latency. Each `uploadFile` SDK call requires 3 sequential pin operations (ciphertext, file metadata, folder metadata), totaling ~4-5s server-side at mean.
123	
124	**Optimization impact (pebbleds datastore):**
125	
126	| Metric               | flatfs (pre) | pebbleds (post) | Change     |
127	| -------------------- | ------------ | --------------- | ---------- |
128	| Pin mean latency     | 1.73s        | 1.37s           | -20.8%     |
129	| Pin p50 (estimated)  | ~1.7s        | ~1.0s           | -41%       |
130	| IPNS publish mean    | ~120ms       | ~50ms           | -58%       |
131	| 75-client errors     | 10           | 0               | Eliminated |
132	| 75-client throughput | 2.44 ops/s   | 3.51 ops/s      | +43.9%     |
133	
134	Concurrent pins from multiple clients cause contention on the Kubo datastore. At 50 clients, throughput plateaus at ~3.4-3.7 ops/s locally and ~15-19 ops/s on staging. At 75 clients with flatfs, errors begin appearing; pebbleds eliminates this failure mode entirely.
135	
136	**Memory and CPU:** Kubo uses Go's goroutine scheduler. Pin operations are I/O-bound on the datastore. The pebbleds LSM-tree datastore batches writes, reducing I/O contention under concurrent load.
137	
138	### 2.2 PostgreSQL
139	
140	Auth lookups, IPNS record cache reads/writes, and vault operations are fast:
141	
142	| Operation        | Mean Latency |
143	| ---------------- | ------------ |
144	| Vault lookup     | < 5ms        |
145	| Quota check      | < 5ms        |
146	| IPNS DB resolve  | 23-36ms      |
147	| IPNS DB upsert   | ~50ms        |
148	| Auth login (JWT) | 104-155ms    |
149	
150	PostgreSQL is **not the bottleneck** at current scale. The `folder_ipns` table grows linearly with `users x folders`, but even at 200 concurrent clients, DB operations remain sub-100ms.
151	
152	**Connection pooling:** TypeORM manages the connection pool. Default pool size handles current concurrency levels without exhaustion.
153	
154	### 2.3 API Server (NestJS)
155	
156	Request processing overhead is minimal (~5-10ms per request excluding downstream IPFS/IPNS calls). The API server acts primarily as a pass-through to Kubo for IPFS operations and to PostgreSQL for IPNS record management.
157	
158	- **Rate limiting:** ThrottlerGuard protects against abuse. Load tests bypass throttling via `THROTTLE_BYPASS_SECRET` in test mode.
159	- **Connection handling:** NestJS handles concurrent HTTP connections efficiently. No observed issues at 200 concurrent clients.
160	- **CPU:** Not a bottleneck. Server-side processing time per request is dominated by Kubo pin latency, not application logic.
161	
162	### 2.4 Redis
163	
164	Used for rate limiting state and session management. Sub-millisecond operations. Not a bottleneck at any observed concurrency level.
165	
166	### 2.5 Someguy (Delegated Routing Sidecar)
167	
168	IPNS publish DHT propagation happens asynchronously and does not block client responses. DHT propagation latency:
169	
170	| Outcome | Mean  | Count   | Error Rate |
171	| ------- | ----- | ------- | ---------- |
172	| Success | 838ms | 147,285 | --         |
173	| Error   | 17.2s | 324     | 0.22%      |
174	
175	DHT propagation errors do not affect client-facing operations (fire-and-forget). Someguy's warm DHT is critical for IPNS resolution performance in non-API contexts (recovery tool, TEE republishing).
176	
177	---
178	
179	## 3. Scaling Recommendations
180	
181	### 3.1 When to Scale
182	
183	| Signal                                     | Threshold              | Action                                     |
184	| ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
185	| Upload p95 > 5s consistently (staging)     | Kubo pin contention    | Add second Kubo node or upgrade hardware   |
186	| Upload p95 > 15s consistently (staging)    | Kubo saturated         | Kubo cluster mode or dedicated pin workers |
187	| API response time p95 > 500ms (non-IPFS)   | API CPU bound          | Add API replica behind load balancer       |
188	| PostgreSQL connections > 80% pool          | DB saturated           | Increase pool size or add read replica     |
189	| Kubo datastore > 80% disk                  | Storage full           | Expand volume or enable garbage collection |
190	| IPNS publish queue depth > 100             | Publish backlog        | Tune Kubo worker concurrency               |
191	| Mixed workload error rate > 1%             | Infrastructure ceiling | Scale horizontally (Kubo + API)            |
192	| Throughput plateaus despite adding clients | Single-node ceiling    | Horizontal scaling required                |
193	
194	### 3.2 How to Scale
195	
196	**Kubo IPFS Node:**
197	
198	- **Vertical:** Increase CPU/RAM/SSD. Pebbleds datastore benefits significantly from faster I/O. Move from HDD to NVMe SSD for pin operations.
199	- **Horizontal:** Run multiple Kubo nodes behind the API. Each API instance pins to a designated Kubo node. Requires content routing coordination (IPFS Cluster or custom routing).
200	- **Cluster mode:** [IPFS Cluster](https://cluster.ipfs.io/) adds pinning coordination but introduces operational complexity. Recommended only when single-node Kubo reaches sustained saturation.
201	
202	**API Server (NestJS):**
203	
204	- The API is stateless (session state in Redis, no in-memory state). Adding replicas behind Caddy or nginx is straightforward.
205	- Ensure all replicas share the same Redis instance for rate limiting and session consistency.
206	- Database connection pool per replica needs coordination (total connections = replicas x pool_size).
207	
208	**PostgreSQL:**
209	
210	- **Read replicas:** IPNS cache reads can be directed to read replicas. Write scaling is unnecessary at current volume.
211	- **Connection pooling:** Use PgBouncer if connection count exceeds PostgreSQL's `max_connections`.
212	- **Sharding:** Premature for a tech demo. Only consider if user count exceeds ~100,000.
213	
214	**TEE Republishing:**
215	
216	- Independent scaling path. Add TEE workers to increase IPNS republish batch throughput.
217	- Each TEE worker processes a batch of IPNS names every 6 hours.
218	- Batch duration grows linearly with IPNS name count. Monitor `cipherbox_republish_batch_duration_seconds`.
219	
220	---
221	
222	## 4. Growth Projections
223	
224	### 4.1 Storage Growth
225	
226	All files are encrypted with unique keys, so IPFS deduplication does not apply.
227	
228	**Formula:**
229	
230	```text
231	storage_bytes = users * avg_files_per_user * avg_encrypted_file_size * 1.05
232	```
233	
234	The 1.05 multiplier accounts for ~5% metadata overhead (file metadata CIDs, folder metadata CIDs, IPNS records).
235	
236	| Users  | Files/User | Avg File Size | IPFS Storage | Monthly Growth (10 files/user/mo) |
237	| ------ | ---------- | ------------- | ------------ | --------------------------------- |
238	| 100    | 100        | 500KB         | ~5 GB        | ~500 MB/mo                        |
239	| 1,000  | 100        | 500KB         | ~50 GB       | ~5 GB/mo                          |
240	| 10,000 | 100        | 500KB         | ~500 GB      | ~50 GB/mo                         |
241	| 1,000  | 100        | 5MB           | ~500 GB      | ~50 GB/mo                         |
242	
243	### 4.2 IPNS Name Growth
244	
245	Each user has:
246	
247	- 1 vault blob IPNS name
248	- N folder IPNS names (1 per folder including root)
249	- M file IPNS names (1 per file)
250	
251	**Formula:**
252	
253	```text
254	ipns_names = users * (1 + avg_folders_per_user + avg_files_per_user)
255	```
256	
257	| Users  | Folders/User | Files/User | Total IPNS Names | TEE Republish Batch Size |
258	| ------ | ------------ | ---------- | ---------------- | ------------------------ |
259	| 100    | 10           | 100        | ~11,100          | ~11,100 every 6h         |
260	| 1,000  | 10           | 100        | ~111,000         | ~111,000 every 6h        |
261	| 10,000 | 10           | 100        | ~1,110,000       | ~1,110,000 every 6h      |
262	
263	TEE republish batch duration scales linearly with IPNS name count. At current Someguy publish throughput (~15 ops/s), 111,000 names would take ~2 hours to republish -- within the 6-hour window. At 1.1M names, multiple TEE workers or batching optimizations would be needed.
264	
265	### 4.3 Database Growth
266	
267	| Table         | Growth Driver             | Rows at 1,000 Users | Rows at 10,000 Users |
268	| ------------- | ------------------------- | ------------------- | -------------------- |
269	| users         | 1 per user                | 1,000               | 10,000               |
270	| vaults        | 1 per user                | 1,000               | 10,000               |
271	| folder_ipns   | users x (folders + files) | ~111,000            | ~1,110,000           |
272	| device_tokens | users x avg_devices       | ~2,000              | ~20,000              |
273	
274	The `folder_ipns` table is the largest growth driver. With proper indexing (already in place on `user_id`, with a unique constraint on `(user_id, ipns_name)`), queries remain efficient at millions of rows.
275	
276	### 4.4 Cost Estimates (Single Server Deployment)
277	
278	These are rough estimates for a self-hosted tech demo deployment, not production SLAs.
279	
280	| Component     | 100 Users      | 1,000 Users     | 10,000 Users     |
281	| ------------- | -------------- | --------------- | ---------------- |
282	| VPS (CPU/RAM) | $10-20/mo      | $40-80/mo       | $150-300/mo      |
283	| Storage (SSD) | $5/mo (50GB)   | $25/mo (250GB)  | $100/mo (1TB)    |
284	| Bandwidth     | $5/mo          | $20/mo          | $100/mo          |
285	| Domain/SSL    | $15/yr         | $15/yr          | $15/yr           |
286	| **Total**     | **~$20-30/mo** | **~$85-125/mo** | **~$350-500/mo** |
287	
288	**Notes:**
289	
290	- VPS pricing based on Hostinger/Hetzner tier estimates
291	- Storage assumes SSD volumes at ~$0.10/GB/mo
292	- Bandwidth depends heavily on download patterns and provider egress pricing
293	- Does not include TEE infrastructure costs (Phala Cloud pricing varies)
294	
295	---
296	
297	## 5. Load Test Thresholds
298	
299	Automated pass/fail thresholds are defined in `tests/load/src/harness/thresholds.ts` and integrated into all 8 load test scenarios. Thresholds are 2-3x observed baselines to avoid CI flakiness while still catching significant regressions.
300	
301	| Scenario           | Operation    | p95 Threshold | Error Rate Max | Rationale                                 |
302	| ------------------ | ------------ | ------------- | -------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
303	| upload-throughput  | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 5%             | 19.2 p95 was 2,841ms at 5 clients (~3.5x) |
304	| mixed-workload     | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 10%            | Mixed has higher error rate historically  |
305	| mixed-workload     | createFolder | 5,000ms       | 10%            | 19.2 p95 was 936ms at 5 clients (~5x)     |
306	| ipns-publish-storm | createFolder | 10,000ms      | 10%            | IPNS contention scenario, high variance   |
307	| sustained-load     | uploadFile   | 10,000ms      | 5%             | Same as upload-throughput                 |
308	| sustained-load     | createFolder | 5,000ms       | 5%             | Folder ops should stay fast               |
309	| spike-test (burst) | uploadFile   | 15,000ms      | 15%            | Spike: intentional overload               |
310	| spike-test (burst) | createFolder | 15,000ms      | 15%            | Spike: intentional overload               |
311	
312	**How thresholds work:**
313	
314	1. Each scenario runs its workload and collects `OperationMetrics` via `aggregateAndReport()`
315	2. `checkThresholds()` compares observed p95 latency and error rate against the threshold table
316	3. If any threshold is breached, the test fails with a descriptive violation message listing the operation, observed value, and threshold
317	4. The CI workflow (`load-test.yml`) runs via `workflow_dispatch` (manual trigger), not on every push
318	
319	**Updating thresholds:** When baselines shift significantly (new infrastructure, new optimizations), update the threshold values in each scenario file. Keep thresholds at 2-3x observed values to maintain a buffer against normal variance.
320	
321	---
322	
323	## 7. Retention Consequence of BYO Advisory Rows
324	
325	### 7.1 Background
326	
327	CipherBox supports two storage modes for each file CID in `pinned_cids`:
328	
329	- **Hosted rows**: CIDs pinned via the CipherBox relay (`isByoUser = false`). The CipherBox
330	  Kubo node physically holds the content.
331	- **BYO advisory rows**: CIDs registered by BYO-IPFS users (`isByoUser = true`). These rows
332	  track what the user claims is pinned on their own infrastructure; the CipherBox Kubo node
333	  does NOT hold these CIDs.
334	
335	### 7.2 The Retention Consequence
336	
337	The `guardedUnpin` refcount query counts ALL `pinned_cids` rows for a given CID regardless of
338	origin — both hosted and BYO advisory rows contribute to the refcount (D-07 design, WR-07
339	disposition: accepted).
340	
341	**Consequence:** If a BYO advisory row exists for a CID that is also held by the CipherBox Kubo
342	node (hosted), deleting the hosted row will NOT trigger a physical unpin of the CID from Kubo
343	until the BYO advisory row is also removed. The CID is retained in Kubo for as long as any BYO
344	advisory row references it.
345	
346	**When this occurs:**
347	
348	1. User A uploads a file via the CipherBox relay (hosted `pinned_cids` row created).
349	2. User B (BYO-IPFS mode) registers the same CID (advisory `pinned_cids` row created).
350	3. User A deletes their file — their hosted row is removed and their quota is decremented
351	   immediately (D-03), but the CID remains pinned in Kubo because User B's advisory row
352	   keeps the refcount above zero.
353	4. Kubo storage is freed only when User B also removes their advisory row.
354	
355	**Impact on capacity planning:** In environments with significant BYO-IPFS usage, Kubo storage
356	consumption may exceed what is attributable to hosted users alone. Monitor
357	`cipherbox_pending_unpin_queue_depth` and `cipherbox_drift_orphaned_pins_total` for divergence
358	between DB-accounted and Kubo-actual pin counts.
359	
360	**Operator action:** No action is required — this is intentional design. If an operator needs
361	to reclaim Kubo storage held by a BYO-referenced CID, they must first remove the BYO advisory
362	row (e.g., by having the BYO user delete their reference), which will allow the next
363	`drain-pending-unpins` BullMQ run to physically unpin it.
364	
365	---

> AGENT

**Latest stable is Kubo v0.42.0** (2026-06-08); we're on **v0.40.0**, so this spans 0.41 → 0.42. Two things make this more than a one-line bump:

1. **It's wide** — `ipfs/kubo:v0.40.0` appears in 2 compose files, **6 CI workflows** (incl. `--filter ancestor=ipfs/kubo:v0.40.0` in `web-e2e.yml`/`deploy-landing.yml`, which must match the running image exactly), and 2 docs. All must move in lockstep. (I'll leave `.planning/` history alone.)
2. **It carries migration risk** — 0.40 already FATAL'd us on a deprecated config key. 0.41/0.42 could do the same or bump the fs-repo version (one-way migration).

So I'm vetting the 0.41/0.42 changelogs before bumping (sub-agent launched), and since I have staging access I'll validate the v0.42.0 image boots cleanly with our `Provide.Strategy: roots` config before the PR merges. Launching the vet + reading the staging compose to start the edits:

> TOOL

```json
  "prompt": "Research the upgrade risk of bumping Kubo (go-ipfs) from v0.40.0 to v0.42.0 for a production-ish deployment. We are jumping across v0.41.0 and v0.42.0.\n\nAuthoritative sources to fetch (use WebFetch/WebSearch):\n- https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/releases/tag/v0.41.0\n- https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/releases/tag/v0.42.0\n- The changelog files: https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/blob/master/docs/changelogs/v0.41.md and https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/blob/master/docs/changelogs/v0.42.md (try the raw URLs if the blob view is hard to parse: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ipfs/kubo/master/docs/changelogs/v0.41.md and .../v0.42.md)\n\nOur current setup that MUST keep working:\n- Docker image ipfs/kubo, env IPFS_PROFILE=server,pebbleds (pebbleds LSM-tree datastore).\n- Config: Provide.Strategy=roots (Kubo 0.40 renamed Reprovider.Strategy -> Provide.Strategy; we already migrated and REMOVED the deprecated Reprovider.* keys from the repo config).\n- We rely on the Provide/Reprovide system and DHT announcement of pin roots.\n- A persistent named volume holds the fs-repo (we do NOT want a surprise one-way repo migration to silently break a rollback).\n\nReport (concise markdown, do NOT write files) covering, for v0.41.0 and v0.42.0 SPECIFICALLY:\n1. BREAKING CHANGES — especially any config key that is deprecated/removed/renamed and causes a FATAL on boot if present (the way Reprovider did in 0.40). List each with the exact key and required action.\n2. fs-repo version / migration — does 0.41 or 0.42 bump the repo version requiring an automatic (one-way) migration on first boot? Is it automatic in the docker image? Any downgrade caveats.\n3. Changes to the Provide / Reprovide / DHT-announcement system — anything that affects Provide.Strategy=roots semantics, new Provide.* keys, new defaults, the \"sweeping DHT provider\" / accelerated provide work, etc.\n4. pebbleds / Datastore changes that affect server,pebbleds profile.\n5. Go version, default config, gateway/RPC changes that could affect a node behind a reverse proxy with API/gateway on loopback.\n6. Any NEW recommended config for our use case (e.g. if 0.41/0.42 introduced a better provide strategy or a knob to reduce provider CPU).\n\nEnd with a clear VERDICT: is bumping 0.40.0 -> 0.42.0 safe for our config as-is, what (if any) config/init changes are required first, and whether a one-way repo migration will occur. Cite the source for each breaking-change claim. Read-only; do not modify files or run anything."
}
```

tool_use Write
id: toolu_01WNmUchnyELTWiMc4eMFTQL
```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/docker/ipfs-init/001-provide-strategy.sh",
  "content": "#!/bin/sh\n# CipherBox Kubo init: announce only pin roots to the DHT, not every block.\n#\n# Reduces background provide/reprovide CPU on the shared Kubo node. With the\n# default \"all\" strategy the staging node scheduled ~76k CIDs for DHT\n# announcement, consuming a large share of Kubo's CPU at idle; \"roots\" cuts that\n# to the pin-root count (~18k) with no loss of retrievability (every pinned root\n# is still announced; only unpinned intermediate blocks are dropped).\n#\n# Kubo 0.40 renamed Reprovider.Strategy -> Provide.Strategy. This file is sourced\n# by the Kubo entrypoint on every boot (before the daemon starts), so the setting\n# is idempotent and survives container recreation and fresh repos.\nipfs config Provide.Strategy roots || echo \"ipfs-init: failed to set Provide.Strategy roots (continuing)\"\n"
}
```

```
  ipfs:
    image: ipfs/kubo:v0.40.0
    container_name: cipherbox-ipfs

    ports:

      # TODO: Create environment-specific configs for staging/prod with:

    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD-SHELL', 'ipfs id || exit 1']
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 10
      start_period: 30s

          # Kubo is load-bearing; mirror the staging tuning (see docs/CAPACITY.md).
          memory: 3G
          cpus: '1.5'

### staging ###
  ipfs:
    image: ipfs/kubo:v0.40.0
    restart: unless-stopped
    environment:
      # pebbleds: LSM-tree datastore for faster concurrent write throughput (Phase 19.2)
      # Requires fresh IPFS repo -- delete ipfs_staging volume on first deploy:

      IPFS_PROFILE: server,pebbleds
    ports:
      - '4001:4001/tcp'
      - '4001:4001/udp'
      - '127.0.0.1:5001:5001'
      - '127.0.0.1:8080:8080'
    volumes:
      - ipfs_staging:/data/ipfs
      # Kubo runs /container-init.d/*.sh on every boot (sourced before the daemon);
      # 001-provide-strategy.sh sets Provide.Strategy=roots idempotently.
      - ./ipfs-init:/container-init.d:ro
    logging: *default-logging
    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD-SHELL', 'ipfs id || exit 1']
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 10
      start_period: 30s
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          # Kubo is load-bearing and shares the 2-vCPU box with someguy. Load
          # testing (2026-06) showed the 1.0 cap pegged under concurrent uploads;
          # 1.5 is the measured throughput knee (+18%), 2.0 gave no further gain.
          memory: 3G
          cpus: '1.5'

  tee-worker:
```

Remaining for #1 is gated on the Kubo changelog vet (still running): apply the v0.40→v0.42 bump across the 10 active references, validate the v0.42 image boots cleanly on staging with our roots config, then write the `CAPACITY.md` update and open the PR. Waiting on the vet now.

> AGENT

Vet came back **green**: bumping 0.40→0.42 is safe for our config — **no fatal-on-boot key** (the one 0.42 boot-refusal is gated on `Provide.DHT.Interval=0`, which we don't set), **no fs-repo migration** (`RepoVersion=18` in both, verified in source), and **rollback-safe** at the format level. Bonus: v0.42's pinner-lock fix stops the reprovide cycle from blocking `pin`/`add` under the `roots` strategy — directly helpful for our load. One caveat: v0.41 does an **irreversible** provider-keystore relocation, so the vet recommends a volume snapshot first.

One thing jumped out while reading `CAPACITY.md`: it documents the staging baseline box as **"4 vCPU, 8GB"**, but the current box reports **`nproc=2`**. If the box was halved from the baseline, that alone explains most of the 15→10 throughput drop — and makes "restore the box to 4 vCPU" the real fix. Confirmed: **current staging box is 2 vCPU / 7.8 GiB** (AMD EPYC 9355P KVM VM). `CAPACITY.md` records the baseline box as **4 vCPU / 8 GB**. So the box was **halved** since the baselines — that's the dominant cause of the 15.10→~10 ops/s drop, and it makes "restore to 4 vCPU" the real fix (where `cpus=1.5` + roots finally pay off uncontended). I'll surface this in my wrap-up and document it now.

> AGENT

Confirmed — thanks for the plan card. The box is a **2 vCPU / 8 GB / 100 GB NVMe / 8 TB** plan. That **retracts my "downsized from 4 vCPU" theory** — this is the plan, so I won't assert downsizing. The `CAPACITY.md` "4 vCPU baseline" line is then most likely a stale/inaccurate doc note (or the 15.10 baseline ran on different hardware) — I'll flag it as unverified rather than claim a regression from core loss.

Net effect on the conclusion: on **this** 2-core plan, the ceiling is ipfs + someguy sharing 2 cores (~10 ops/s at 50 clients). Two levers beat that — **upgrade the plan to ≥4 vCPU** (highest leverage; if you're looking at the plan page to do exactly that, the data says it recovers toward 15/s) or **cut per-upload someguy work** (the #3 lead). And on disk: you're using 4.1 G of 100 G, so **cleanup (#4) is pure hygiene, not space pressure**.

```json
  "old_string": "**Mean pin latency:** 1.37s (down from 1.73s pre-optimization, -20.8%)\n\n---",
  "new_string": "**Mean pin latency:** 1.37s (down from 1.73s pre-optimization, -20.8%)\n\n### 1.5 Re-baseline (2026-06): provider CPU and someguy contention\n\nA 2026-06 staging re-baseline (upload-throughput @ 50 clients) measured ~10 ops/s versus the 15.10 ops/s recorded in Phase 19.2 (§1.2). Object count was ruled out as the cause: a garbage-collection from 294,811 to 20,038 datastore objects did not change throughput.\n\nCPU-limit sweep (all runs share the same datastore state, `Provide.Strategy=roots`, on the 2 vCPU staging host):\n\n| ipfs cpus | someguy cpus | uploadFile p50 | uploadFile p95 | Throughput  |\n| --------- | ------------ | -------------- | -------------- | ----------- |\n| 1.0       | 1.0          | 5,779ms        | 8,001ms        | 8.71 ops/s  |\n| 1.5       | 1.0          | 4,894ms        | 6,506ms        | 10.28 ops/s |\n| 2.0       | 1.0          | 5,105ms        | 7,385ms        | 9.80 ops/s  |\n| 2.0       | 0.4          | 5,855ms        | 7,501ms        | 8.66 ops/s  |\n\nFindings:\n\n- **Two cores are the wall.** During load, ipfs and someguy together saturate the host's two cores (combined CPU 160-176% of 200%). Raising ipfs past 1.5 gives nothing (2.0 ties 1.5) because the physical cores, shared with someguy, are the ceiling. 1.5 is the measured knee (+18% over 1.0).\n- **someguy is a per-upload participant, not a parasite.** Each upload publishes two IPNS records through the API's delegated-routing client (someguy): one per-file record and one parent-folder record. Throttling someguy to 0.4 made throughput _worse_ (8.66 ops/s) by bottlenecking the publish path - it is on the critical path and must not be starved.\n- **Object count is not the throughput driver** (GC test above). It affects background provide/DHT CPU, which `Provide.Strategy=roots` already bounds to the pin-root count (~18k roots vs ~76k blocks under the default `all`).\n- **Hardware note:** the host is a 2 vCPU / 8 GB / 100 GB NVMe plan. §1.1 records a 4 vCPU baseline host; that figure is unverified and inconsistent with the current plan, so cross-baseline comparisons against the 15.10 ops/s figure may not be same-hardware.\n\nApplied remediation (PR): Kubo upgraded v0.40.0 -> v0.42.0, ipfs limits raised to `cpus: 1.5` / `memory: 3G`, `Provide.Strategy=roots` made reproducible via a `/container-init.d` script. Recommended next: upgrade the host to >= 4 vCPU (or move someguy to its own cores) and defer the per-file IPNS publish off the upload critical path (§3.2).\n\n---"
}
```

Ah — that's the clincher: if **4 vCPU + 8 GB isn't a Hostinger plan**, then the `CAPACITY.md` "4 vCPU, 8GB baseline" is a **flat doc error**, not a different box. So the box **is and always was 2 vCPU / 8 GB**, which means the 15.10 ops/s Phase 19.2 baseline was on **this same hardware** — and the drop to ~10 ops/s is a **same-hardware, software/contention regression**, not core loss.

That makes **#3 (cut per-upload someguy publishes) the real same-hardware fix**, and a plan upgrade (Hostinger's next KVM tier is **4 vCPU / 16 GB**) the way to lift the 2-core ceiling beyond baseline. Correcting the doc accordingly — fixing the bogus 4-vCPU line and reframing as same-hardware:

Someguy's warm DHT is critical for IPNS resolution performance in non-API contexts (recovery tool, TEE republishing).\n\n**Per-upload load (2026-06):** someguy is also on the upload critical path - each `uploadFile` publishes two IPNS records through it (per-file + parent-folder), so its CPU tracks upload volume (spiking to 80-90% under 50-client load). Throttling it _reduces_ upload throughput (§1.5). Note someguy is a read/proxy delegated-routing _resolver_ and cannot accept provide writes, so Kubo's content announcing cannot be offloaded to it; reducing per-upload publishes (§3.2) is the lever, not delegating providing to someguy.\n\n---"
```json
}
```

```json
}
```
